# Daily Summary: 2025-06-13

### One For All: LLM-based Heterogeneous Mission Planning in Precision Agriculture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10106v1)
- **Authors**: Marcos Abel Zuzuárregui, Mustafa Melih Toslak, Stefano Carpin
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence is transforming precision agriculture, offering farmers new tools to streamline their daily operations. While these technological advances promise increased efficiency, they often introduce additional complexity and steep learning curves that are particularly challenging for non-technical users who must balance tech adoption with existing workloads. In this paper, we present a natural language (NL) robotic mission planner that enables non-specialists to control heterogeneous robots through a common interface. By leveraging large language models (LLMs) and predefined primitives, our architecture seamlessly translates human language into intermediate descriptions that can be executed by different robotic platforms. With this system, users can formulate complex agricultural missions without writing any code. In the work presented in this paper, we extend our previous system tailored for wheeled robot mission planning through a new class of experiments involving robotic manipulation and computer vision tasks. Our results demonstrate that the architecture is both general enough to support a diverse set of robots and powerful enough to execute complex mission requests. This work represents a significant step toward making robotic automation in precision agriculture more accessible to non-technical users.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an extension of a previously developed system using Large Language Models (LLMs) for robotic mission planning in precision agriculture. The key contribution is adapting the architecture to control *heterogeneous* robots – specifically, both a wheeled robot and a manipulator arm – using a common natural language interface. The system leverages LLMs to translate user intent into executable mission plans defined by XML schemas, allowing non-technical users to control complex tasks like object detection and manipulation, and autonomous navigation in orchards. The system incorporates a software validation engine with an XSD-based architecture to guarantee that the mission planner provides an action in a known syntax. The results demonstrate the feasibility of the approach in field experiments, highlighting both the capabilities and limitations of using LLMs for spatial reasoning and kinematic control in agricultural settings. The paper emphasizes modularity and the ability to integrate custom software for specific tasks like "Next Best View" pose generation, showcasing the potential to combine LLM-based planning with existing robotic capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *generalization* of an existing LLM-based mission planning architecture to support multiple *heterogeneous* robots in a practical agricultural setting. While using LLMs for robotic control isn't new (as the paper itself acknowledges with numerous citations), this work makes a concrete contribution by demonstrating that such an architecture can be expanded to a new set of robots with different capabilities and tasks, while retaining the user-friendly natural language interface. The modular software architecture, incorporating an XSD validation engine to ensure the returned plans are valid, is a noteworthy aspect of the contribution.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it directly addresses a real-world problem: the complexity of using diverse robotic systems in precision agriculture. The user base in this domain often lacks the technical expertise to program or operate robots with specific interfaces. The LLM-based system offers a path towards democratizing robotic automation in agriculture by providing a common and intuitive control interface. By doing this in an environment that is both difficult (outdoor, unstructured, partially connected) and ecologically significant, the authors demonstrate the value of this approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Application:** The paper focuses on a practical and relevant application (precision agriculture) with clear potential benefits.
    *   **Heterogeneous Robot Support:** Extends to a multi-robot system with multiple capabilities and robots.
    *   **Modular Architecture:** The modular design allows for easier integration of new robots and custom software components.
    *   **Emphasis on Robustness:**  The use of XSD schema validation and an associated validation engine helps to address the inherent uncertainties of LLM output.
    *   **Clear Identification of Limitations:** The paper honestly acknowledges the limitations of LLMs in spatial reasoning and kinematic control and proposes solutions (modularizing those complex tasks) instead of overstating capabilities.
    *   **Links to Established Standards:** The paper adheres to established standards such as IEEE, and provides a strong Related Work section to explain the value add.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM's Black-Box Nature:** The reliance on a "black-box" LLM like ChatGPT means that the system's behavior is difficult to fully predict or control. The success depends heavily on prompt engineering.
    *   **Limited Kinematic Reasoning Improvement:** The solution for kinematic reasoning (modularity) is more of a workaround than a fundamental improvement in the LLM's spatial understanding. It pushes the complexity to other software modules.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Evaluation beyond Semantic Correctness:** The experiments focus mainly on *semantic* success, i.e., whether the LLM-generated plans are syntactically valid and represent the user's intent. Quantitative metrics of the robotic performance (e.g., task completion time, accuracy of object detection, energy efficiency) are not provided. There is an implication that the semantics are related to the metrics through successful experiments, but the direct correlation is never addressed.
    *   **Scalability:** The paper does not provide a clear indication of how the system would scale with a much larger fleet of robots or a more complex set of agricultural tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in LLM-based robot control, particularly in domains where ease of use is paramount. The modular architecture and emphasis on robustness are valuable contributions that could be adopted by other researchers. The work also highlights the need for further research on improving LLMs' spatial reasoning and kinematic control capabilities.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper provides a valuable extension to an existing system for LLM-based robot control. The focus on *heterogeneous* robot systems and a real-world agricultural application gives the work a practical relevance that increases its value. The limitations associated with spatial reasoning and the lack of quantitative results, prevent a higher score. The integration with existing software and standards in the field, however, gives the paper a significant edge and makes it of high value in the field. Overall, the paper presents solid engineering and a clear understanding of both the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs for robotic mission planning. The code is also publicly available (see link in paper), which increases replicability, adoption, and hence its value to the wider robotics and automation community.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### AI5GTest: AI-Driven Specification-Aware Automated Testing and Validation of 5G O-RAN Components
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10111v1)
- **Authors**: Abiodun Ganiyu, Pranshav Gajjar, Vijay K Shah
- **Abstract**: The advent of Open Radio Access Networks (O-RAN) has transformed the telecommunications industry by promoting interoperability, vendor diversity, and rapid innovation. However, its disaggregated architecture introduces complex testing challenges, particularly in validating multi-vendor components against O-RAN ALLIANCE and 3GPP specifications. Existing frameworks, such as those provided by Open Testing and Integration Centres (OTICs), rely heavily on manual processes, are fragmented and prone to human error, leading to inconsistency and scalability issues. To address these limitations, we present AI5GTest -- an AI-powered, specification-aware testing framework designed to automate the validation of O-RAN components. AI5GTest leverages a cooperative Large Language Models (LLM) framework consisting of Gen-LLM, Val-LLM, and Debug-LLM. Gen-LLM automatically generates expected procedural flows for test cases based on 3GPP and O-RAN specifications, while Val-LLM cross-references signaling messages against these flows to validate compliance and detect deviations. If anomalies arise, Debug-LLM performs root cause analysis, providing insight to the failure cause. To enhance transparency and trustworthiness, AI5GTest incorporates a human-in-the-loop mechanism, where the Gen-LLM presents top-k relevant official specifications to the tester for approval before proceeding with validation. Evaluated using a range of test cases obtained from O-RAN TIFG and WG5-IOT test specifications, AI5GTest demonstrates a significant reduction in overall test execution time compared to traditional manual methods, while maintaining high validation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the AI5GTest paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AI5GTest, an AI-driven framework for automated testing and validation of 5G O-RAN components. AI5GTest aims to address the limitations of existing manual and fragmented O-RAN testing processes by automating test case execution, validating signaling messages against 3GPP and O-RAN specifications, and providing root cause analysis for test failures.  The framework utilizes a cooperative Large Language Model (LLM) approach with three main components: Gen-LLM (procedural flow generation), Val-LLM (specification compliance validation), and Debug-LLM (root cause analysis). The paper presents an experimental evaluation of AI5GTest using a 5G O-RAN testbed, showcasing its ability to reduce testing time and maintain high validation accuracy compared to manual methods.  A key feature is a human-in-the-loop mechanism in Gen-LLM to ensure accuracy and transparency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the integration of LLMs into the O-RAN testing workflow.  Specifically, the automated generation of expected procedural flows from specifications (Gen-LLM) and the intelligent validation of signaling messages (Val-LLM & Debug-LLM) is a departure from traditional manual approaches. While there is existing research into using AI for network management and security testing, this paper specifically targets *procedural validation* in the context of multi-vendor O-RAN components, which is a specific gap.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper is potentially high.  The proliferation of O-RAN components from diverse vendors introduces complex testing challenges that current manual approaches struggle to address efficiently and consistently. AI5GTest offers a potential solution for automating and scaling O-RAN testing, which is vital for wider adoption and deployment of O-RAN networks.  Faster testing and validation cycles could accelerate innovation and reduce time-to-market for new O-RAN components. However, this depends on a few factors:

*   **Generalizability:**  The evaluation is conducted using the srsRAN open-source stack. While the authors claim platform-agnostic design, thorough validation with commercial O-RAN components is necessary to demonstrate practical utility.
*   **Performance with complex scenarios:**  The evaluation focuses on a limited set of test cases. It's unclear how AI5GTest would scale or perform with more complex, real-world scenarios or with test cases that involve multiple interacting components and configurations. The paper also doesn't delve into the specifics of its test case generation capabilities or its ability to identify edge cases.
*   **LLM Accuracy and Dependence:** The framework relies heavily on the LLMs. Any limitations or inaccuracies of the LLMs would directly impact the framework's reliability. It's important to discuss the potential issues of LLM hallucinations or misinterpretations in the context of O-RAN specifications. Also, while leveraging open source models is good, a dependence on a specific LLM (e.g., Mistral-7B) makes the solution prone to issues if there are changes to that model.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a critical problem:** O-RAN testing is a recognized bottleneck, and the paper tackles it directly.
*   **Novel approach:** The integration of LLMs for procedural validation is innovative.
*   **Complete Framework:** Provides a complete methodology, from generation to validation and debugging.
*   **Human-in-the-loop:** The mechanism allows test engineers to verify results and provide feedback to the system.
*  **Code and Datasets provided** They provide access to the codebase and packet traces.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation scope is relatively constrained and relies on simulations for certain failure scenarios. Real-world, multi-vendor testing is necessary.
*   **Dependence on Open-Source Stack:** Limited external validity beyond srsRAN.
*   **Lack of Detailed Complexity Analysis:** The performance evaluation lacks a detailed analysis of the computational complexity of the AI algorithms, specifically LLM inference, which can be quite significant for larger O-RAN deployments.
*   **Unclear details on test case generation:** More detail needs to be provided. How does this generate new test cases to uncover edge cases as the framework currently relies on existing test cases.

**Potential Influence:**

If successful, AI5GTest or similar AI-driven approaches could significantly transform O-RAN testing and validation, reducing costs, accelerating deployment, and improving network reliability. It could also serve as a blueprint for applying AI to other aspects of network management and security.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially impactful approach to a significant problem in the O-RAN domain. The integration of LLMs into the validation workflow is promising. However, the current evaluation is limited, and certain aspects require further clarification and more rigorous validation in diverse, real-world O-RAN environments. The reliance on open-source and specific LLMs creates some concerns about the general applicability and long-term viability of the proposed framework. A better evaluation of the framework in edge cases and complex validation scenarios is needed. For these reasons, a score of 7 reflects the paper's strong potential while acknowledging the areas that require further investigation.

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ChartReasoner: Code-Driven Modality Bridging for Long-Chain Reasoning in Chart Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10116v1)
- **Authors**: Caijun Jia, Nan Xu, Jingxuan Wei, Qingli Wang, Lei Wang, Bihui Yu, Junnan Zhu
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities through long-chain reasoning before responding. However, how to extend this capability to visual reasoning tasks remains an open challenge. Existing multimodal reasoning approaches transfer such visual reasoning task into textual reasoning task via several image-to-text conversions, which often lose critical structural and semantic information embedded in visualizations, especially for tasks like chart question answering that require a large amount of visual details. To bridge this gap, we propose ChartReasoner, a code-driven novel two-stage framework designed to enable precise, interpretable reasoning over charts. We first train a high-fidelity model to convert diverse chart images into structured ECharts codes, preserving both layout and data semantics as lossless as possible. Then, we design a general chart reasoning data synthesis pipeline, which leverages this pretrained transport model to automatically and scalably generate chart reasoning trajectories and utilizes a code validator to filter out low-quality samples. Finally, we train the final multimodal model using a combination of supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning on our synthesized chart reasoning dataset and experimental results on four public benchmarks clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed ChartReasoner. It can preserve the original details of the charts as much as possible and perform comparably with state-of-the-art open-source models while using fewer parameters, approaching the performance of proprietary systems like GPT-4o in out-of-domain settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "ChartReasoner: Code-Driven Modality Bridging for Long-Chain Reasoning in Chart Question Answering" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChartReasoner, a two-stage framework for chart question answering (ChartQA) that aims to bridge the gap between visual information and symbolic reasoning. The first stage, Chart2Code, trains a model to convert chart images into structured ECharts code, preserving layout and data semantics. The second stage leverages this code to synthesize ChartThink, a large-scale chart reasoning dataset. The final ChartReasoner model is trained using supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning to improve reasoning accuracy, consistency, and interpretability. Experiments on public benchmarks show the effectiveness of ChartReasoner, performing competitively with open-source models and approaching the performance of proprietary systems like GPT-40 in out-of-domain settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a code-driven approach is novel. Previous methods often rely on image-to-text transformations that discard crucial structural information. By translating charts into ECharts code, the paper preserves details and provides a symbolic representation suitable for reasoning. The two-stage framework is also well-designed, separating chart understanding from reasoning, which allows for modular improvements. The construction of the ChartThink dataset is another significant contribution.
*   **Significance:** ChartQA is an important area of research. This method addresses a major limitation in current ChartQA systems: lossy visual transformations. By using ECharts as an intermediary representation, the model has access to precise quantitative and layout information, which enhances reasoning performance. The results demonstrate that the proposed method performs comparably with state-of-the-art open-source models and nears proprietary models in out-of-domain settings, indicating good potential for generalizability.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The code-driven approach allows for more accurate preservation of chart details.
    *   The two-stage framework makes the system modular and potentially easier to improve.
    *   The ChartThink dataset provides a valuable resource for training and evaluating chart reasoning models.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach, with competitive performance on multiple benchmarks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on ECharts code could be a limitation, as not all charts are easily representable in this format.
    *   While the results are promising, the performance gap with proprietary models is still noticeable, especially in in-domain settings.
    *   The paper uses Qwen2.5-VL-7B which is a relatively small model, suggesting there is still room for improvement via scaling.
    *   The dependence on DeepSeek-R1 as a code/reasoning generator could introduce bias.
*   **Potential Influence:** The ChartReasoner framework has the potential to influence future research in ChartQA and visual reasoning. The idea of using symbolic code as an intermediary representation could be applied to other types of visualizations and reasoning tasks. The ChartThink dataset could also serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating new chart reasoning models.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel approach and achieves competitive results, there are still some limitations. The reliance on ECharts code and the performance gap with the best proprietary models prevent a higher score. However, the strong design, dataset contribution, and promising experimental results warrant a favorable evaluation.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Detecção da Psoríase Utilizando Visão Computacional: Uma Abordagem Comparativa Entre CNNs e Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10119v1)
- **Authors**: Natanael Lucena, Fábio S. da Silva, Ricardo Rios
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a comparison of the performance of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) in the task of multi-classifying images containing lesions of psoriasis and diseases similar to it. Models pre-trained on ImageNet were adapted to a specific data set. Both achieved high predictive metrics, but the ViTs stood out for their superior performance with smaller models. Dual Attention Vision Transformer-Base (DaViT-B) obtained the best results, with an f1-score of 96.4%, and is recommended as the most efficient architecture for automated psoriasis detection. This article reinforces the potential of ViTs for medical image classification tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary of the paper and a critical evaluation, including a novelty/significance score:

**Paper Summary**

The paper "Detecção da Psoríase Utilizando Visão Computacional: Uma Abordagem Comparativa Entre CNNs e Vision Transformers" (Psoriasis Detection Using Computer Vision: A Comparative Approach Between CNNs and Vision Transformers) compares the performance of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) for multiclassifying images of psoriasis lesions and similar skin diseases. The study adapts models pre-trained on ImageNet to a specific dataset of skin lesion images. The results show that both CNNs and ViTs achieve high predictive metrics, but ViTs, especially the Dual Attention Vision Transformer-Base (DaViT-B), demonstrate superior performance with smaller models, achieving an F1-score of 96.4%. The paper recommends DaViT-B as the most efficient architecture for automated psoriasis detection and emphasizes the potential of ViTs in medical image classification.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The paper directly compares CNNs and ViTs on a relevant and challenging task. This kind of head-to-head comparison is valuable for the community.
    *   **Strong Performance:** The achieved F1-score of 96.4% with DaViT-B is commendable and demonstrates the potential of these models in a real-world application.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a reasonably detailed description of the dataset creation (though limited by publicly available data), pre-processing steps, training configurations, and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Relevance:** Psoriasis detection is a practical application of computer vision with potential benefits for diagnosis and treatment.
    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** The paper acknowledges the challenges in visual diagnosis of psoriasis due to its similarity to other skin conditions and the subjective nature of the process.
    *   **Expand on Previous Work:** The paper expands upon previous binary classification work by including multiple similar skin conditions and comparing ViTs with CNNs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The dataset, while curated, is limited by reliance on publicly available images. This may introduce biases and limit generalizability to different patient populations or image acquisition settings. The collection process could be more detailed (specific keywords used on Google Images).
    *   **Lack of Dataset Detail:** The paper mentioned data augmentation but lacks clarity on whether it was performed only on the training set, a crucial detail to prevent overfitting. This could significantly impact the reliability of the results.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper compares CNNs and ViTs, the application of these architectures to psoriasis detection isn't entirely new. Prior work exists that uses both. The added value comes from the comparative analysis and the specific architectures tested, but that value is somewhat limited.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Computational Cost:** While the paper mentions the number of parameters, it doesn't explicitly address the training and inference costs (memory usage, time) of the models. This is important for practical deployment.
    *   **Missing Confusion Matrix Explanation:** Although a confusion matrix is presented, there should be detailed explanation of specific errors.
    *   **Limited Baselines:** The paper compares a small number of CNN and ViT architectures. A more comprehensive comparison, including some more recent or state-of-the-art models could be useful.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The novelty is somewhat limited. Similar research in applying deep learning for psoriasis exists, but this paper provides a direct comparison of CNNs and ViTs on a multi-class problem. The significance lies in showcasing the potential of ViTs (specifically DaViT-B) for this task, but the dataset limitations and incremental nature of the work reduce its overall impact.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a comparative analysis, which is helpful for practitioners in the field. However, the limitations related to dataset quality and the fact that the application of deep learning (including ViTs) to skin disease classification is already established, the novelty and significance are somewhat diminished. Although the approach is well-structured and the results are promising, the limitations above and, particularly, the relatively incremental improvement over existing knowledge hold the score back.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### D-LiFT: Improving LLM-based Decompiler Backend via Code Quality-driven Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10125v1)
- **Authors**: Muqi Zou, Hongyu Cai, Hongwei Wu, Zion Leonahenahe Basque, Arslan Khan, Berkay Celik, Dave, Tian, Antonio Bianchi, Ruoyu, Wang, Dongyan Xu
- **Abstract**: Decompilers, which reconstruct human-readable source code from binary executables, are vital to many security tasks. Yet, despite recent advances, their output often suffers from syntactic and semantic errors and remains difficult to read. Recently, with the advent of large language models (LLMs), researchers began to explore the potential of LLMs to refine decompiler output. Nevertheless, our study of these approaches reveals significant limitations, such as introducing new errors and relying on unreliable accuracy validation. In this paper, we present D-LiFT, an automated decompiler backend that harnesses and further trains LLMs to improve the quality of decompiled code via reinforcement learning (RL). Unlike prior work that overlooks preserving accuracy, D-LiFT adheres to a key principle for enhancing the quality of decompiled code: \textit{preserving accuracy while improving readability}. Central to D-LiFT, we propose D-SCORE, an integrated quality assessment system to score the decompiled code from multiple aspects. In line with our principle, D-SCORE assigns low scores to any inaccurate output and only awards higher scores for readability to code that passes the accuracy check. Specifically, D-SCORE first verifies the syntactic and semantic correctness via the compiler and symbolic execution; only if a candidate is deemed accurate, it then evaluates readability using established metrics to compare the LLM output with the original decompiled code. The score will then be fed back to the LLM for fine-tuning. Our implementation, based on Ghidra and a range of LLMs, demonstrates significant improvements for the accurate decompiled code from the coreutils and util-linux projects. Compared to baseline LLMs without D-SCORE-driven fine-tuning, D-LiFT produces 55.3% more improved decompiled functions, as measured by D-SCORE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the D-LIFT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents D-LIFT, an automated decompiler backend that uses reinforcement learning (RL) to improve the quality of decompiled code generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). The key principle behind D-LIFT is to *preserve accuracy* while *improving readability*.  A central component is D-SCORE, a novel code quality assessment system that scores decompiled code from multiple perspectives. D-SCORE verifies syntactic and semantic correctness using a compiler and symbolic execution. Only code passing these checks is then evaluated for readability using established metrics. This score is fed back to the LLM for fine-tuning using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Experiments using Ghidra and various LLMs demonstrate significant improvements in decompiled code from coreutils and util-linux, surpassing baseline LLMs in terms of D-SCORE and overall quality.  The paper highlights an "improve-ability gap," noting LLMs struggle to fix originally inaccurate decompiled code, regardless of fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies in the integrated approach to LLM-based decompilation improvement. Several aspects contribute to this:

*   **D-SCORE Metric:** The primary contribution is the D-SCORE metric.  It combines accuracy checks (compiler and symbolic execution) with readability assessment in a principle manner, placing a high bar on the accuracy and providing more reward for the readability improvement on the valid codes. This is a critical departure from previous LLM-based code generation works, which often overlook the need to maintain program correctness. This is a strong point of the paper.
*   **Reinforcement Learning with GRPO:** Applying RL to decompilation is not entirely new, but the use of GRPO to handle the inherent ambiguity of valid decompilation outputs (multiple semantically equivalent source codes for a given binary) is well-motivated and effectively addresses a key challenge.  This shows a thoughtful adaptation of existing RL techniques to a specific problem domain.
*   **Emphasis on Accuracy:** The paper clearly articulates and consistently emphasizes the importance of preserving accuracy during decompilation improvement. This is a crucial point that differentiates it from prior works like LLM4Decompile, which may prioritize readability at the expense of correctness.

*Significance:*

The paper has the potential for significant impact on the field of reverse engineering and security analysis.  Here's why:

*   **Improved Decompilation Quality:** High-quality decompiled code is essential for various security tasks (vulnerability analysis, reverse engineering, etc.). D-LIFT's improvements in accuracy and readability can directly reduce the effort required for manual analysis.
*   **Addressing LLM Limitations:** The paper identifies and addresses the limitations of using LLMs for code generation, specifically the tendency to introduce errors. D-SCORE and the RL framework help mitigate this.
*   **Practical Framework:** The implementation details and experimental results demonstrate that D-LIFT is a practical framework that can be readily adopted and extended. The modular design makes it easier to integrate different LLMs and decompilers.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Reliance on D-helix:** The accuracy assessment relies heavily on D-helix. While D-helix is a robust tool, its inherent limitations (handling of floating-point operations, large binaries, external function calls) become limitations of D-LIFT as well. More advanced accuracy validation techniques could further improve the framework.
*   **"Improve-ability Gap":** The paper identifies a significant weakness: LLMs struggle to improve *originally inaccurate* decompiled code. While this is an important observation, the paper doesn't offer a solution to this problem. This suggests that future research should focus on improving the decompiler front-end or developing LLMs that are better at correcting errors in decompiled code. This means the current framework can be easily defeated by low-quality decompiled code.
*   **Limited Dataset & Baselines:** While the datasets used in experiments include functions from commonly used benchmark, coreutils and util-linux, the size may not be representative of all possible code structures, especially complex and obfuscated code.  In addition, there is only one related LLM-based work for comparison. Experiments with more diverse and representative datasets will further improve the claim about the improvements of the proposed framework.
*   **GRPO specific hyperparameters:** Equation (2) gives out multiple parameters that needs to be carefully tuned to specific setting. It would be more useful if more details about the hyperparameter tuning process and justification for the selected hyperparameters are included.
*   **Limited External Function Call Validation:** The accuracy checks for external function calls are simplified and might miss certain semantic deviations.

*Justification for Score:*

The paper presents a solid and well-executed approach to improving LLM-based decompilation. The D-SCORE metric, the use of GRPO, and the emphasis on accuracy are key contributions. While the paper does have limitations, particularly concerning the reliance on D-helix and the "improve-ability gap," it significantly advances the state-of-the-art in LLM-assisted decompilation. The framework's practical nature and potential impact on security analysis justify a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### ViCrit: A Verifiable Reinforcement Learning Proxy Task for Visual Perception in VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10128v1)
- **Authors**: Xiyao Wang, Zhengyuan Yang, Chao Feng, Yongyuan Liang, Yuhang Zhou, Xiaoyu Liu, Ziyi Zang, Ming Li, Chung-Ching Lin, Kevin Lin, Linjie Li, Furong Huang, Lijuan Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown great effectiveness for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) using tasks that are challenging yet easily verifiable, such as math reasoning or code generation. However, extending this success to visual perception in vision-language models (VLMs) has been impeded by the scarcity of vision-centric tasks that are simultaneously challenging and unambiguously verifiable. To this end, we introduce ViCrit (Visual Caption Hallucination Critic), an RL proxy task that trains VLMs to localize a subtle, synthetic visual hallucination injected into paragraphs of human-written image captions. Starting from a 200-word captions, we inject a single, subtle visual description error-altering a few words on objects, attributes, counts, or spatial relations-and task the model to pinpoint the corrupted span given the image and the modified caption. This formulation preserves the full perceptual difficulty while providing a binary, exact-match reward that is easy to compute and unambiguous. Models trained with the ViCrit Task exhibit substantial gains across a variety of VL benchmarks. Crucially, the improvements transfer beyond natural-image training data to abstract image reasoning and visual math, showing promises of learning to perceive rather than barely memorizing seen objects. To facilitate evaluation, we further introduce ViCrit-Bench, a category-balanced diagnostic benchmark that systematically probes perception errors across diverse image domains and error types. Together, our results demonstrate that fine-grained hallucination criticism is an effective and generalizable objective for enhancing visual perception in VLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViCrit (Visual Caption Hallucination Critic), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) proxy task designed to enhance visual perception in vision-language models (VLMs). VLMs have seen success with RL fine-tuning in areas like math and code, but visual perception has lagged due to the difficulty of creating challenging yet easily verifiable tasks. ViCrit addresses this by training VLMs to identify subtle, synthetically injected visual hallucinations in detailed, paragraph-length image captions. The task involves a model acting as a critic, pinpointing the corrupted span in the caption given the image. The reward is a binary, exact-match score. The authors demonstrate that VLMs fine-tuned with ViCrit exhibit substantial gains across various VLM benchmarks, including improvements that transfer to abstract image reasoning and visual math.  To facilitate evaluation, they also introduce ViCrit-Bench, a category-balanced diagnostic benchmark to systematically probe perception errors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies in the specific formulation of the RL proxy task *and* the creation of a targeted benchmark. While RL fine-tuning of LLMs and VLMs is not entirely new, the idea of using synthetic hallucination detection as a proxy for general visual perception is clever. Specifically, the method makes the following novel arguments: (1) by focusing on localizing *synthetic* hallucination, rather than caption generation, you can produce unambiguous, binary verification signals for RL training. (2) By focusing on very detailed, *paragraph-length* captions, you can create a perceptual difficulty to improve fine-grained visual reasoning. Other papers have used RL for VLMs, but this particular approach with synthetically injected subtle errors and precise localization is fairly novel and well motivated. The creation of ViCrit-Bench, designed to systematically assess different types of visual hallucinations, further adds to the novelty by providing a focused evaluation framework.

**Significance:** The significance is moderate to high. The results show clear improvements on existing VLM benchmarks and, importantly, a positive transfer to abstract visual reasoning tasks. This suggests that ViCrit isn't just memorizing specific image features, but is enabling a more generalizable form of visual understanding.  The ViCrit-Bench is also a valuable contribution, providing a tool for diagnosing the specific weaknesses of VLMs in visual perception. By addressing the problem of ambiguous evaluation metrics which hinders the use of reinforcement learning, it makes the whole process more accessible. However, there is still room to improve the sophistication of the "visual hallucination" formulation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Task:** ViCrit is a clearly defined and easily implemented task.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements across various benchmarks and model sizes.
*   **Generalization:** The generalization to abstract reasoning tasks is a significant strength.
*   **ViCrit-Bench:** The diagnostic benchmark is a valuable resource for the community.
*   **Clear Motivation:** The problem of ambiguous and hard to verify metrics are well outlined for a VLM reinforcement learning setting.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Hallucinations:** While cleverly designed, the "hallucinations" are synthetic. It's possible that models are learning to detect these artificial errors rather than truly improving their visual perception of *real* hallucinations (e.g., detecting hallucinated objects in generated images or captions). While the paper does show generalization, this point still needs to be emphasized.
*   **Task Complexity:** Although cleverly constructed, the specific formulation may be less practical/applicable. Although carefully constructed, the ViCrit-Bench itself does not contribute any improvements to actual model benchmarks.
*   **Data Imbalance in Training Dataset:** The paper acknowledges a data imbalance in the PixMo-Cap dataset (dominated by natural images), which could affect the generalization performance.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a new approach to RL fine-tuning for VLMs and by highlighting the importance of addressing visual hallucinations. The ViCrit-Bench could become a standard tool for evaluating VLM perception. Other papers can definitely build on the work by addressing some of the limitations of the hallucination generation.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The score reflects the paper's innovative approach to a challenging problem and its strong empirical support. The major upside is the focus on reinforcement learning and a binary signal approach, which lowers the barrier to improve VLM results. While the use of *synthetic* hallucinations is a limitation, the demonstrated generalization suggests that the approach is capturing something meaningful about visual perception. The creation of the benchmark and the clear presentation of results further strengthen the paper. The minor drawback is the sophistication of this particular setup over other approaches. Overall, the paper offers a practical and insightful contribution with strong potential for future development.

### Diffusion prior as a direct regularization term for FWI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10141v1)
- **Authors**: Yuke Xie, Hervé Chauris, Nicolas Desassis
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have recently shown promise as powerful generative priors for inverse problems. However, conventional applications require solving the full reverse diffusion process and operating on noisy intermediate states, which poses challenges for physics-constrained computational seismic imaging. In particular, such instability is pronounced in non-linear solvers like those used in Full Waveform Inversion (FWI), where wave propagation through noisy velocity fields can lead to numerical artifacts and poor inversion quality. In this work, we propose a simple yet effective framework that directly integrates a pretrained Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) as a score-based generative diffusion prior into FWI through a score rematching strategy. Unlike traditional diffusion approaches, our method avoids the reverse diffusion sampling and needs fewer iterations. We operate the image inversion entirely in the clean image space, eliminating the need to operate through noisy velocity models. The generative diffusion prior can be introduced as a simple regularization term in the standard FWI update rule, requiring minimal modification to existing FWI pipelines. This promotes stable wave propagation and can improve convergence behavior and inversion quality. Numerical experiments suggest that the proposed method offers enhanced fidelity and robustness compared to conventional and GAN-based FWI approaches, while remaining practical and computationally efficient for seismic imaging and other inverse problem tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to full waveform inversion (FWI) that leverages denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) as a direct regularization term. Instead of using the full reverse diffusion process that involves operating on noisy intermediate states (which can lead to instability, particularly in FWI), the authors propose integrating a pre-trained DDPM denoiser directly into the FWI update rule using a score rematching strategy. This simplifies the process, avoids operating in the noisy latent space, reduces the number of iterations needed, and operates entirely within the clean image space. This promotes stable wave propagation, enhances convergence, and improves inversion quality. The authors demonstrate the efficacy of their method on synthetic seismic data, showing improved fidelity and robustness compared to conventional and GAN-based FWI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the *direct* integration of the DDPM denoiser as a regularization term within the FWI framework, avoiding the full reverse diffusion sampling process. Existing work has explored diffusion models for inverse problems, including seismic imaging, but typically relies on sampling from the reverse diffusion process, which introduces noise and stability challenges. The score-rematching technique is also not entirely new, but its application within FWI in this manner is.  The method simplifies diffusion model usage within FWI and addresses limitations of prior techniques. The idea of score matching for incorporating diffusion priors isn't new, but the application to FWI and the specific way it bypasses noisy latent space manipulations make it a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** FWI is a crucial technique for subsurface imaging. Improving its stability, convergence, and accuracy has significant implications for oil and gas exploration, geothermal energy, carbon sequestration, and other geophysical applications. The proposed method offers a promising avenue for enhancing FWI performance. Avoiding the instability inherent in reverse diffusion sampling and the challenges associated with GAN training are substantial advantages. The paper provides a computationally efficient and practical approach that can be readily integrated into existing FWI pipelines. However, the paper still relies on synthetic data and the generalization ability needs to be addressed more thoroughly in the discussion.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with a detailed explanation of the proposed approach.
    *   Thorough experimental validation on synthetic data, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing methods.
    *   Addresses a critical limitation of standard diffusion model use in FWI: the instability introduced by noisy latent space sampling.
    *   The method is relatively simple to implement, making it accessible to researchers and practitioners in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited generalization tests to real-world data or more complex synthetic datasets (e.g., incorporating more complex geological features or noise). The Marmousi2 dataset test is helpful, but not exhaustive.
    *   The paper does not delve deeply into the selection of hyperparameters, such as the weighting factor for the regularization term. The sensitivity of the method to these hyperparameters is not discussed.
    *   While it is mentioned that the method could be extended to elastic FWI or uncertainty quantification, these extensions are not explored in detail.
    *   The computational cost is addressed but a more detailed performance comparison to conventional FWI implementations would strengthen the results.
    *   The choice of U-Net architecture is not justified and the experiments are not rigorously designed (lacking ablations, statistical significance tests, hyperparameter sweeps, etc.)

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the FWI community by providing a more stable, efficient, and accurate approach to incorporating generative priors. This could lead to improved subsurface models and better decision-making in various geophysical applications. The simplicity of the method is an advantage that could facilitate adoption by practitioners. The limitations that are described, however, would need to be addressed for a greater impact.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses outlined above, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field of FWI. While the method has clear advantages over existing approaches, the limited experimental validation and lack of detailed analysis of hyperparameters prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.  The paper is well-written and the method is clearly explained, adding to its accessibility.  The potential for impact is high given the practical nature of the proposed approach, but further research and validation are required.

### RoCA: Robust Cross-Domain End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10145v1)
- **Authors**: Rajeev Yasarla, Shizhong Han, Hsin-Pai Cheng, Litian Liu, Shweta Mahajan, Apratim Bhattacharyya, Yunxiao Shi, Risheek Garrepalli, Hong Cai, Fatih Porikli
- **Abstract**: End-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has recently emerged as a new paradigm, offering significant potential. However, few studies have looked into the practical challenge of deployment across domains (e.g., cities). Although several works have incorporated Large Language Models (LLMs) to leverage their open-world knowledge, LLMs do not guarantee cross-domain driving performance and may incur prohibitive retraining costs during domain adaptation. In this paper, we propose RoCA, a novel framework for robust cross-domain E2E autonomous driving. RoCA formulates the joint probabilistic distribution over the tokens that encode ego and surrounding vehicle information in the E2E pipeline. Instantiating with a Gaussian process (GP), RoCA learns a set of basis tokens with corresponding trajectories, which span diverse driving scenarios. Then, given any driving scene, it is able to probabilistically infer the future trajectory. By using RoCA together with a base E2E model in source-domain training, we improve the generalizability of the base model, without requiring extra inference computation. In addition, RoCA enables robust adaptation on new target domains, significantly outperforming direct finetuning. We extensively evaluate RoCA on various cross-domain scenarios and show that it achieves strong domain generalization and adaptation performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ROCA: Robust Cross-Domain End-to-End Autonomous Driving":

**Summary:**

The paper presents ROCA, a new framework designed to improve the robustness and cross-domain generalization of end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving systems. Instead of relying on large language models, ROCA uses a Gaussian Process (GP) to learn a codebook of basis tokens representing diverse driving scenarios. During inference, the GP probabilistically predicts future trajectories based on the similarity between the current scene's token embedding and the learned basis tokens. This probabilistic approach allows ROCA to handle unseen scenarios and estimate prediction uncertainty. The uncertainty is used to improve training by weighting the loss function based on the level of uncertainty. The framework is adaptable, allowing for finetuning, active learning, and online adaptation.  Experiments demonstrate improved domain generalization and adaptation performance on the nuScenes dataset, across different cities and under various image degradations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a Gaussian Process (GP) to learn and leverage a codebook of basis tokens for cross-domain adaptation in end-to-end autonomous driving is reasonably novel.  While GPs have been used in other domain adaptation tasks, their application to E2E driving with a focus on handling long-tail events through uncertainty estimation is a distinct contribution. The specific combination of a learned codebook, GP-based probabilistic inference, and uncertainty-weighted training loss is a notable element of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in autonomous driving: deploying models trained in one domain (e.g., city) to another. E2E models are known to be brittle and lack the generalization capabilities of traditional modular systems. ROCA tackles this directly by focusing on domain generalization and adaptation, which is crucial for practical deployment. The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in both accuracy and robustness, suggesting the potential for real-world impact. The ability to perform active learning by identifying the most informative data for finetuning further enhances its practical value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Approach:** The GP-based approach offers a principled way to model uncertainty, which is then used to improve training and enable active learning. This distinguishes it from ad-hoc approaches to domain adaptation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation across multiple cross-domain scenarios (city-to-city transfer, image degradation) and with different base models, providing strong evidence of the effectiveness of ROCA.
    *   **Adaptability:** ROCA's support for finetuning, active learning, and online adaptation makes it a versatile framework for different deployment scenarios.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the ROCA framework, its underlying principles, and its experimental validation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Base Model:** ROCA relies on the ability of a base E2E model to extract meaningful tokens. The performance of ROCA is therefore limited by the representational power of the chosen base model. The sensitivity to the chosen architecture needs further investigation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper claims that ROCA doesn't require extra inference computation in some configurations, the version that uses the GP module for trajectory prediction *does* increase computational cost. A more detailed analysis of the computational trade-offs would be beneficial.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The nuScenes dataset, while widely used, is still limited in its diversity compared to real-world driving scenarios. Evaluating ROCA on a more challenging and diverse dataset would strengthen the conclusions.
    *  **Lack of comparison with other methods:** There is a lack of comparison with other popular cross domain AD methods like Domain Adaptation via style transfer, as well as other feature alignment approaches.
*   **Potential Influence:** ROCA has the potential to influence the field of autonomous driving by providing a practical and effective solution for cross-domain generalization. Its modular design and adaptability make it potentially attractive for researchers and practitioners working on E2E driving systems. The GP approach could inspire further research into uncertainty-aware training and adaptation techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

While ROCA presents a solid contribution with novel ideas and comprehensive experiments, some limitations prevent it from achieving a higher score. The dependency on a base model, the increased computational cost in certain configurations, the dataset limitations, and the lack of comparisons restrict its practical significance and potential influence on the field.

### When Large Language Models are Reliable for Judging Empathic Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10150v1)
- **Authors**: Aakriti Kumar, Nalin Poungpeth, Diyi Yang, Erina Farrell, Bruce Lambert, Matthew Groh
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at generating empathic responses in text-based conversations. But, how reliably do they judge the nuances of empathic communication? We investigate this question by comparing how experts, crowdworkers, and LLMs annotate empathic communication across four evaluative frameworks drawn from psychology, natural language processing, and communications applied to 200 real-world conversations where one speaker shares a personal problem and the other offers support. Drawing on 3,150 expert annotations, 2,844 crowd annotations, and 3,150 LLM annotations, we assess inter-rater reliability between these three annotator groups. We find that expert agreement is high but varies across the frameworks' sub-components depending on their clarity, complexity, and subjectivity. We show that expert agreement offers a more informative benchmark for contextualizing LLM performance than standard classification metrics. Across all four frameworks, LLMs consistently approach this expert level benchmark and exceed the reliability of crowdworkers. These results demonstrate how LLMs, when validated on specific tasks with appropriate benchmarks, can support transparency and oversight in emotionally sensitive applications including their use as conversational companions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how reliably Large Language Models (LLMs) can judge empathic communication in text-based conversations. It compares LLM annotations with expert and crowdworker annotations across four different evaluative frameworks from psychology, NLP, and communication studies. The authors analyze inter-rater reliability between these groups, using expert agreement as a benchmark. They find that LLMs consistently approach the level of expert reliability and exceed the reliability of crowdworkers across these frameworks. The paper demonstrates that LLMs, with appropriate benchmarks, can support transparency and oversight in emotionally sensitive applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Question:** The paper addresses a critical but underexplored area:  the reliability of LLMs *as judges* of nuanced communication, rather than simply generators of it. This is particularly important considering the increasing use of LLMs in sensitive areas like mental health support.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The study employs a robust methodology, systematically comparing LLM performance with expert and crowdworker annotations across multiple established frameworks and conversational contexts.  The use of inter-rater reliability metrics (Krippendorff's alpha and Cohen's weighted kappa) and detailed analysis of sub-component agreement provide a much richer understanding of LLM performance than standard classification metrics would.
    *   **Meaningful Benchmarking:** Using expert agreement as a benchmark is crucial. It avoids the problematic assumption that a single 'ground truth' exists in subjective annotation tasks, recognizing inherent ambiguity and contextuality.
    *   **Identification of Limitations:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of crowd-sourced annotations and potential biases, acknowledging the need for specialized knowledge in evaluating nuanced communication.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have immediate implications for deploying LLMs ethically and responsibly in emotionally sensitive domains. The study provides a roadmap for validating LLMs on specific tasks with appropriate benchmarks, improving transparency and accountability.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** The paper offers not only quantitative results but also qualitative analyses (example conversations) that help clarify where disagreements arise and how different annotator groups interpret communication.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Contextual Constraints:** While the study analyzes multiple conversational contexts, they are all text-based and limited to interactions between strangers.  The findings may not directly generalize to more complex, longitudinal relationships.
    *   **Framework Dependency:** The results are inherently tied to the specific evaluative frameworks used. The reliance on existing frameworks might limit the exploration of other potentially relevant aspects of empathic communication.
    *   **Limited Expertise:** While the “experts” have relevant backgrounds, the extent and nature of their training aren’t extensively detailed (aside from noting one leads workshops and the other trained with them).  More detail on their qualification and adherence to a coding process could enhance the validity.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The focus on LLM *judgement* of empathic communication is a departure from the more common focus on LLM generation capabilities, adding a new dimension to LLM evaluation.
    *   The study challenges the traditional use of crowd-sourced annotations as a cost-effective ground truth, highlighting the importance of expertise in tasks requiring nuanced understanding.
    *   The paper advances a more rigorous approach to LLM validation, emphasizing the use of appropriate benchmarks and inter-rater reliability metrics, which is directly relevant to ethical AI deployment.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   This paper is likely to influence future research on evaluating LLMs for social and emotional intelligence, encouraging more nuanced and context-aware assessment methodologies.
    *   It may prompt the development of more specialized training data and frameworks for LLMs intended to function as conversational companions.
    *   The study could contribute to policy discussions regarding the responsible deployment of LLMs in sensitive domains, advocating for rigorous validation and human oversight.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** This paper is a significant contribution to the field. It addresses a timely and important question using a rigorous methodology and provides valuable insights into the reliability of LLMs as judges of empathic communication. While the study has some limitations regarding contextual constraints and detailed expertise descriptions, its strengths and potential impact on the field warrant a high score. The focus on judgment, the benchmarking methodology, and the demonstration of LLMs' relatively high inter-rater reliability with experts are all substantial contributions. The recommendations for future research and responsible deployment are also highly valuable. The potential to inform policy discussions related to emotionally-aware AI further increases its significance. A perfect score is not assigned due to the identified limitations of generalizability and the potential to enhance expertise documentation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can LLMs Generate Good Stories? Insights and Challenges from a Narrative Planning Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10161v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Wang, Max Kreminski
- **Abstract**: Story generation has been a prominent application of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, understanding LLMs' ability to produce high-quality stories remains limited due to challenges in automatic evaluation methods and the high cost and subjectivity of manual evaluation. Computational narratology offers valuable insights into what constitutes a good story, which has been applied in the symbolic narrative planning approach to story generation. This work aims to deepen the understanding of LLMs' story generation capabilities by using them to solve narrative planning problems. We present a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on narrative planning based on literature examples, focusing on causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict. Our experiments show that GPT-4 tier LLMs can generate causally sound stories at small scales, but planning with character intentionality and dramatic conflict remains challenging, requiring LLMs trained with reinforcement learning for complex reasoning. The results offer insights on the scale of stories that LLMs can generate while maintaining quality from different aspects. Our findings also highlight interesting problem solving behaviors and shed lights on challenges and considerations for applying LLM narrative planning in game environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Can LLMs Generate Good Stories? Insights and Challenges from a Narrative Planning Perspective."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate high-quality stories by framing story generation as a narrative planning problem.  Instead of relying on subjective human evaluations or superficial automatic metrics, the authors use insights from computational narratology, specifically focusing on causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict. They create a benchmark of narrative planning problems derived from literature examples, scaling them up using parameterized variations and Answer Set Programming (ASP) for automatic validation. They test GPT-4 tier LLMs on these tasks, finding that while LLMs can handle causal soundness at smaller scales, character intentionality and dramatic conflict present significant challenges and often require reinforcement learning for more complex reasoning.  The study highlights limitations related to maintaining quality at larger scales and provides insights into the problem-solving behaviors of LLMs in the context of classical planning, shedding light on challenges and engineering practices needed to apply LLM narrative planning in game environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty by explicitly drawing connections between computational narratology, specifically symbolic narrative planning techniques, and the capabilities of LLMs. Most work to this point was about generating story text directly. This paper shifts the perspective to using LLMs to solve the underlying *planning* problems of narrative generation, which is a valuable contribution to the field. Creating the benchmark, and especially parameterizing the instances and using ASP for validation, is a strong aspect of the research and greatly improves the rigor and scalability.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its rigorous approach to evaluating LLMs for story generation. Moving beyond simple text generation metrics and subjective human evaluation to objective measures grounded in narrative theory provides a valuable framework for future research. The insights into specific challenges, such as maintaining character intentionality and handling conflicts, are useful for guiding future LLM development and engineering practices. This is especially relevant for game development and other interactive narrative domains where LLMs are increasingly being used. Furthermore, the negative result (LLMs struggle with intentionality and conflict planning at scale) is also valuable.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Framing:** The paper clearly frames story generation as a narrative planning problem, providing a well-defined context for evaluation.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of parameterized benchmarks, ASP for automatic validation, and systematic experimentation strengthens the validity of the findings.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The paper provides valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in generating stories, with practical implications for game development and interactive narrative.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of the benchmark taskset, ASP encoding, and LLM prompts increases the reproducibility of the study and facilitates future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the focus on causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict is valuable, it is also a limited scope. Other aspects of story quality, such as emotional impact, theme, and style, are not considered.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The paper relies on prompting to elicit narrative plans from LLMs. The effectiveness of prompting is heavily dependent on the specific design of prompts. Further investigation could be done to explore the impact of varying prompting styles and strategies.
    *   **Limited LLM Testing:** While the evaluation includes GPT-4 tier models and Claude models, exploring a broader range of LLMs with different architectures and training data could strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Scale is Still Limited:** Although the paper scales up existing examples to create new instances, the scales are still limited compared to the types of planning and story generation requirements in real-world game environments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM-based story generation by providing a rigorous framework for evaluation and highlighting specific challenges. It could encourage the development of new techniques for improving LLMs' ability to handle character intentionality and dramatic conflict, as well as the exploration of hybrid approaches that combine LLMs with symbolic narrative planning techniques. It's also likely to spur more work in the neuro-symbolic approaches integrating LLMs with planning. It can also influence narrative design for games, helping designers understand the strengths and weaknesses of using LLMs for story generation.

* **Rigorous Rationale for the Score Assigned**:

The paper makes a significant contribution by bridging computational narratology and LLM-based story generation. The methodology is rigorous, the insights are valuable, and the results are clearly presented. The authors identified a critical need for objective benchmarks in story generation and produced a valuable new resource.  While it's not a *groundbreaking* conceptual leap, the practical value and rigor of the work raise its score substantially. The paper offers a robust framework and identified some key weaknesses of current LLMs, pushing future research towards a more grounded and useful approach. The weaknesses are relatively minor and mainly concern the limitations of a single study. The score is penalized by the relatively small scale of the experiments relative to realistic games. However, the scale of tasks are relatively larger than that explored in previous papers.
