- **Score**: 7/10

### PDESpectralRefiner: Achieving More Accurate Long Rollouts with Spectral Adjustment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10711v1)
- **Authors**: Li Luo, Shangsong Liang
- **Abstract**: Generating accurate and stable long rollouts is a notorious challenge for time-dependent PDEs (Partial Differential Equations). Recently, motivated by the importance of high-frequency accuracy, a refiner model called PDERefiner utilizes diffusion models to refine outputs for every time step, since the denoising process could increase the correctness of modeling high frequency part. For 1-D Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equation, refiner models can degrade the amplitude of high frequency part better than not doing refinement process. However, for some other cases, the spectrum might be more complicated. For example, for a harder PDE like Navior-Stokes equation, diffusion models could over-degrade the higher frequency part. This motivates us to release the constraint that each frequency weighs the same. We enhance our refiner model with doing adjustments on spectral space, which recovers Blurring diffusion models. We developed a new v-prediction technique for Blurring diffusion models, recovering the MSE training objective on the first refinement step. We show that in this case, for different model backbones, such as U-Net and neural operators, the outputs of PDE-SpectralRefiner are more accurate for both one-step MSE loss and rollout loss.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PDESpectralRefiner: Achieving More Accurate Long Rollouts with Spectral Adjustment":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of achieving accurate and stable long-term rollouts in time-dependent Partial Differential Equations (PDEs).  Building upon the PDERefiner, which utilizes diffusion models to refine outputs at each time step to improve high-frequency accuracy, the authors propose PDESpectralRefiner. This enhanced model incorporates spectral adjustments to the denoising process of the diffusion model.  Specifically, it allows for frequency-dependent control over the noise introduced during refinement, effectively recovering blurring diffusion models.  The authors introduce a novel v-prediction technique tailored for their spectral adjustment approach, which helps in resuming MSE training. The experimental results demonstrate that PDESpectralRefiner outperforms PDERefiner on several PDE problems, particularly in recovering high frequencies, for models like U-Nets and neural operators. It is applied to Navier Stokes equation, where the original refiner underperforms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the introduction of frequency-specific spectral adjustment within a diffusion-based PDE solver.  While PDERefiner uses diffusion models, PDESpectralRefiner extends this by explicitly controlling the denoising process in the spectral domain.  The blurring v-prediction technique, necessary to facilitate the recovery of MSE training objectives, is another contribution, albeit more technical. Recovering blurring diffusion models adds flexibility to the network. The choice of parameters for spectral adjustment is left to user to be determined. The experiments are limited in scope and depth; more detailed comparisons and sensitivity analysis are needed.

* **Significance:**  Accurate and stable long rollouts are crucial for many scientific and engineering applications involving PDEs. The PDESpectralRefiner presents a promising approach to improve the performance of neural PDE solvers in this respect, especially for more complicated problems that require handling the frequencies. By addressing the over-degradation or inadequate refinement of high frequencies, the method could potentially lead to more reliable and physically realistic simulations. If the design of spectral adjustment becomes more automated, then it will significantly improve the models. 

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenge of high-frequency accuracy in PDE solvers and how it affects long rollouts.
    * **Well-Motivated Approach:** The use of spectral adjustment is logically motivated by the limitations of standard diffusion models in handling complex spectral characteristics of PDEs.
    * **Technical Contribution:** The blurring v-prediction technique addresses a specific issue arising from the spectral adjustment, contributing to a more complete and functional method.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results, although limited, show demonstrable improvement over PDERefiner on the Navier-Stokes equation, which is a strong supporting point.
    * **Generalization**: The method works with multiple backbones such as U-Net and neural operators.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Experiments:** The experiments primarily focus on the 2D Navier-Stokes equation with a single dataset and a limited exploration of different parameter settings. More extensive benchmarking on various PDE problems and datasets would strengthen the claims. The 1-D case is only put in the appendix.
    * **Lack of Automation**: The determination of the spectral adjustment weights is largely manual and requires domain expertise. A method for automating or learning these weights would significantly enhance the practical applicability of the approach.
    * **FNO integration**: The authors state that "PDE-SpectralRefiner still outperforms PDE-Refiner in this case, proving that FNOs could have a better coordination with blurring diffusion models with the specific blurring schedule". However, the results do not support this statement.
    * **Hyperparameter Tuning**: The method requires more hyperparameter tuning than other algorithms.
    * **Clarity**: It would be helpful to have a diagram that explains the method.

* **Potential Influence:** PDESpectralRefiner is potentially influential in the field of physics-informed machine learning. It advances the state-of-the-art in PDE solvers by addressing a critical issue in long-term rollouts, It also opens up avenues for further research such as automating the spectral adjustment process, investigating different spectral parameterizations, and exploring the application of the method to a wider range of PDE problems. The analysis section has value insights for understanding the differences between 1D/2D equations.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The PDESpectralRefiner presents a clear advancement over existing diffusion-based PDE solvers with promising results on a difficult benchmark, however, the approach currently requires manual configuration of spectral parameters and has a limited set of experiments, thus limiting its real-world impact. A rigorous justification is provided throughout the assessment.

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Inferring Adjective Hypernyms with Language Models to Increase the Connectivity of Open English Wordnet
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10715v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Augello, John P. McCrae
- **Abstract**: Open English Wordnet is a key resource published in OntoLex-lemon as part of the linguistic linked open data cloud. There are, however, many links missing in the resource, and in this paper, we look at how we can establish hypernymy between adjectives. We present a theoretical discussion of the hypernymy relation and how it differs for adjectives in contrast to nouns and verbs. We develop a new resource for adjective hypernymy and fine-tune large language models to predict adjective hypernymy, showing that the methodology of TaxoLLaMa can be adapted to this task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the under-explored area of adjective hypernymy within lexical semantics, specifically focusing on improving the connectivity of the Open English Wordnet (OEWN). It presents a theoretical framework for understanding hypernymy between adjectives, acknowledging its inherent complexity and context-dependency compared to noun and verb hypernymy. The authors construct a gold-standard English dataset of hyponym-hypernym pairs, drawing inspiration from other language WordNets (Polish and Dutch) that explicitly model adjective hypernymy. They then evaluate the performance of two large language models (TaxoLLaMa and SmolLM-360M-Instruct) on this task, both in zero-shot settings and after fine-tuning on their new dataset. The experiments investigate the models' ability to identify adjective hypernyms, considering factors like POS tagging and semantic disambiguation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in addressing adjective hypernymy directly. This contrasts with the significant body of work focused on nouns and verbs. The construction of a gold-standard dataset for English adjective hypernymy is a valuable contribution. While borrowing ideas from Polish and Dutch WordNets isn't entirely original, the adaptation and validation for English within the OEWN framework is a significant effort. The prompt engineering and the multiple synonym approach in the dataset are relatively simple but contribute to capturing semantic nuance.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from several aspects:

    *   **Theoretical Clarity:** By explicitly addressing the nuances of adjective hypernymy, the paper contributes to a more comprehensive theory of lexical relations.
    *   **Resource Creation:** The gold-standard dataset, even with its limited size, is a tangible resource for future research on adjective semantics.
    *   **LLM Evaluation:** Evaluating LLMs on this specific task sheds light on their ability to capture subtle semantic relationships and exposes potential biases or limitations.
    *   **OEWN Enhancement:** The ultimate goal of improving OEWN's connectivity has practical implications for the broader Linguistic Linked Open Data (LLOD) cloud.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly defined problem:** The paper effectively highlights the gap in research on adjective hypernymy.
    *   **Sound methodology:** The approach is well-structured, encompassing theoretical discussion, dataset construction, and experimental evaluation.
    *   **Careful annotation:**  The multi-annotator approach with inter-annotator agreement analysis increases the dataset's reliability. The inclusion of definitions for disambiguation adds rigor.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The paper provides thorough analysis of the results, including the impact of different prompting strategies, fine-tuning approaches, and model architectures.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Size:** The relatively small size of the gold-standard dataset (302 pairs) is a significant limitation. This restricts the generalizability of the findings and the potential for effective fine-tuning of LLMs.
    *   **Model Selection:** While TaxoLLaMa is a reasonable choice, the selection of SmolLM-360M-Instruct seems less motivated, especially since LLMs have continued to develop rapidly in the intervening year. Evaluating more recent, larger models would enhance the paper's impact.
    *   **Limited LLM analysis:** While the performance changes after fine-tuning are documented, a more in-depth qualitative analysis of the LLMs' errors and the specific types of adjective relationships they struggle with would provide deeper insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging more focused work on adjective semantics.
    *   Providing a benchmark dataset for evaluating LLMs on adjective hypernymy.
    *   Motivating the expansion of adjective hypernymy in lexical resources like OEWN.
    *   Guiding the development of more semantically aware language models that can better handle the complexities of adjective meaning.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses an important gap and makes a tangible contribution through dataset creation, the limited dataset size and model selection are significant weaknesses. The theoretical framework, while sound, doesn't present groundbreaking ideas. The experimental results are interesting, but their generalizability is constrained by the dataset's limitations. Therefore, a score that acknowledges the paper's contribution while reflecting its limitations is appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### PREMISE: Scalable and Strategic Prompt Optimization for Efficient Mathematical Reasoning in Large Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10716v1)
- **Authors**: Ye Yu, Yaoning Yu, Haohan Wang
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) such as Claude 3.7 Sonnet and OpenAI o1 achieve strong performance on mathematical benchmarks using lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, but the resulting traces are often unnecessarily verbose. This inflates token usage and cost, limiting deployment in latency-sensitive or API-constrained settings. We introduce PREMISE (PRompt-based Efficient Mathematical Inference with Strategic Evaluation), a prompt-only framework that reduces reasoning overhead without modifying model weights. PREMISE combines trace-level diagnostics with gradient-inspired prompt optimization to minimize redundant computation while preserving answer accuracy. The approach jointly optimizes brevity and correctness through a multi-objective textual search that balances token length and answer validity. Unlike prior work, PREMISE runs in a single-pass black-box interface, so it can be applied directly to commercial LLMs. On GSM8K, SVAMP, and Math500 we match or exceed baseline accuracy ($96\%\rightarrow96\%$ with Claude, $91\%\rightarrow92\%$ with Gemini) while reducing reasoning tokens by up to $87.5\%$ and cutting dollar cost by $69$--$82\%$. These results show that prompt-level optimization is a practical and scalable path to efficient LRM inference without compromising reasoning quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PREMISE (PRompt-based Efficient Mathematical Inference with Strategic Evaluation), a novel prompt-only framework designed to enhance the efficiency of mathematical reasoning in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) without modifying model weights. PREMISE uses trace-level diagnostics to identify and mitigate overthinking and underthinking by combining gradient-based prompt optimization and multi-objective optimization based on natural language gradients. The framework encourages concise reasoning by penalizing long traces while preserving accuracy. The method is evaluated across GSM8K, SVAMP, and Math500 datasets, demonstrating that it can match or exceed the accuracy of standard Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting while substantially reducing reasoning token usage and cost, particularly in commercial LLMs like Claude, Gemini, and (with some caveats) OpenAI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Applicability:** The most significant strength is its focus on prompt-based optimization suitable for black-box commercial LLMs (Claude, Gemini, etc.) where model weights are inaccessible. This significantly broadens its potential impact. This contrasts with many existing methods that require fine-tuning or other model-level modifications.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The combination of trace-level diagnostics (overthinking/underthinking metrics) with a gradient-based prompt optimization strategy is innovative. It introduces a more principled way to control reasoning length compared to simple length constraints or heuristics.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments across three standard datasets (GSM8K, SVAMP, Math500) demonstrate the effectiveness of PREMISE in improving token efficiency while maintaining (or sometimes improving) accuracy.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Study:** The ablation studies shed light on the importance of multi-objective optimization, showing the trade-offs between accuracy and token efficiency.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** The authors provide insight into how PREMISE works by analyzing examples of verbose vs. concise reasoning. They also discuss the limitations and potential mitigations, showing a strong understanding of the framework's behavior.
    *   **Information Density:** The paper identifies and addresses the information density problem, which results from inefficient reasoning by agents, that affects the speed and accuracy of task scheduling.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Model Behavior:** The effectiveness of PREMISE is contingent on how well the LLM responds to the structured prompt and the compression objective. The results with OpenAI (o1) demonstrate that models aligned to reward verbose "self-reflection" can be resistant to the framework.
    *   **Scalability of Gradients:** While the gradient-based optimization is performed in the textual space, the scalability of these gradients to much larger or more complex tasks is not explicitly addressed. There might be difficulties in optimizing for more complex reasoning tasks where the gradient signal is weak or noisy.
    *   **Over-Compression Potential:** The reduction in accuracy on the more complex Math-500 dataset when using Gemini suggests a potential for over-compression, leading to the omission of necessary steps. The paper mentions adaptive compression, but this requires further development.
    *   **Assumptions about 'Correct' Traces:** The definition of overthinking relies on the existence of a shortest correct trace *L*<sup>*</sup>(q), which may not be unique or easily determined in practice for more complex reasoning scenarios.

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of trace-level diagnostics with a gradient-based optimization method applied to prompts, all within a black-box setting. Prior work has addressed efficient reasoning, but PREMISE offers a more systematic and adaptable approach.

*   **Significance:** The significance comes from the practical implications of reducing token usage and cost for commercial LLM deployments. This makes sophisticated reasoning more accessible and feasible in resource-constrained or latency-sensitive environments.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence research on prompt engineering and efficient reasoning in LLMs, particularly in commercial settings. It provides a valuable framework for diagnosing and addressing inefficiencies in LLM reasoning traces.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's focus on black-box optimization and the novel use of trace-level diagnostics in prompt engineering are strong contributions with practical significance. The empirical validation and detailed analysis support the claims. However, limitations remain regarding model-specific behavior, scaling gradient-based optimization, and the potential for over-compression. The practical applications and novel methodology warrant a score on the higher end.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TaxoAdapt: Aligning LLM-Based Multidimensional Taxonomy Construction to Evolving Research Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10737v1)
- **Authors**: Priyanka Kargupta, Nan Zhang, Yunyi Zhang, Rui Zhang, Prasenjit Mitra, Jiawei Han
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of scientific fields introduces challenges in organizing and retrieving scientific literature. While expert-curated taxonomies have traditionally addressed this need, the process is time-consuming and expensive. Furthermore, recent automatic taxonomy construction methods either (1) over-rely on a specific corpus, sacrificing generalizability, or (2) depend heavily on the general knowledge of large language models (LLMs) contained within their pre-training datasets, often overlooking the dynamic nature of evolving scientific domains. Additionally, these approaches fail to account for the multi-faceted nature of scientific literature, where a single research paper may contribute to multiple dimensions (e.g., methodology, new tasks, evaluation metrics, benchmarks). To address these gaps, we propose TaxoAdapt, a framework that dynamically adapts an LLM-generated taxonomy to a given corpus across multiple dimensions. TaxoAdapt performs iterative hierarchical classification, expanding both the taxonomy width and depth based on corpus' topical distribution. We demonstrate its state-of-the-art performance across a diverse set of computer science conferences over the years to showcase its ability to structure and capture the evolution of scientific fields. As a multidimensional method, TaxoAdapt generates taxonomies that are 26.51% more granularity-preserving and 50.41% more coherent than the most competitive baselines judged by LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TaxoAdapt, a novel framework for automatically constructing multi-dimensional taxonomies from scientific literature using Large Language Models (LLMs). TaxoAdapt addresses the limitations of existing methods (either corpus-driven or LLM-based) by dynamically adapting an LLM-generated taxonomy to a specific corpus, taking into account the multiple dimensions a research paper can contribute to (e.g., methodology, tasks, evaluation metrics). The framework employs hierarchical text classification to identify and expand relevant nodes in the taxonomy, incorporating both depth and width expansions based on the topical distribution within the corpus. Experimental results on computer science conferences demonstrate that TaxoAdapt generates more granularity-preserving, coherent, and relevant taxonomies compared to baselines. The paper also presents case studies showing how the framework captures the evolution of research topics over time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the dynamic adaptation of LLM-based taxonomies to specific corpora across multiple dimensions. Existing methods often rely solely on either general knowledge from LLMs or on a specific corpus, neglecting the dynamic nature of evolving scientific fields and the multi-faceted nature of scientific contributions. TaxoAdapt's hierarchical classification-based expansion signals and taxonomy-aware clustering are novel contributions.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in organizing and retrieving rapidly growing scientific literature. By automating taxonomy construction with better alignment to specific domains and capturing the evolution of topics, the method has the potential to improve search engines, knowledge discovery, and research workflows. The demonstrated improvements in granularity, coherence, and relevance metrics highlight the practical value of TaxoAdapt.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies and addresses the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The TaxoAdapt framework is well-defined and modular, making it relatively easy to understand.
    *   The use of hierarchical classification and taxonomy-aware clustering for dynamic adaptation is innovative.
    *   The empirical evaluation is comprehensive, with comparisons to strong baselines and ablation studies.
    *   The inclusion of case studies provides concrete examples of how the method captures the evolution of research topics.
    * The framework demonstrates robustness across different datasets, dimension, and even more specialized non-CS domains.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on the quality of the LLM used for initial taxonomy construction and classification.
    *   The framework parameters (e.g., density threshold, maximum depth) may require tuning for different domains and corpora.
    *   Although a human evaluation validates automated metrics, a more extensive assessment of the downstream applications (e.g., search, knowledge discovery) would further strengthen the claims.
    * The paper has limitations as TaxoAdapt relies on LLMs to classify papers into specific dimensions and this classification relies on parametric knowledge of LLMs, which could be limited when LLMs' knowledge is outdated.
*   **Potential Influence:** TaxoAdapt can influence future research in automated taxonomy construction by emphasizing the importance of dynamic adaptation and multi-dimensional analysis. It can also stimulate research on integrating LLM-based knowledge with corpus-specific information for knowledge discovery. The method's modular design could inspire new approaches for exploring and organizing scientific literature.

**Justification of Score:**

TaxoAdapt represents a significant advancement in automatic taxonomy construction for scientific literature. It addresses key limitations of existing methods and provides a practical framework with demonstrated improvements in performance and adaptability. While there is still room for improvement in the evaluation and robustness, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field. 

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Integrating Large Language Models into Text Animation: An Intelligent Editing System with Inline and Chat Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10762v1)
- **Authors**: Bao Zhang, Zihan Li, Zhenglei Liu, Huanchen Wang, Yuxin Ma
- **Abstract**: Text animation, a foundational element in video creation, enables efficient and cost-effective communication, thriving in advertisements, journalism, and social media. However, traditional animation workflows present significant usability barriers for non-professionals, with intricate operational procedures severely hindering creative productivity. To address this, we propose a Large Language Model (LLM)-aided text animation editing system that enables real-time intent tracking and flexible editing. The system introduces an agent-based dual-stream pipeline that integrates context-aware inline suggestions and conversational guidance as well as employs a semantic-animation mapping to facilitate LLM-driven creative intent translation. Besides, the system supports synchronized text-animation previews and parametric adjustments via unified controls to improve editing workflow. A user study evaluates the system, highlighting its ability to help non-professional users complete animation workflows while validating the pipeline. The findings encourage further exploration of integrating LLMs into a comprehensive video creation workflow.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel LLM-aided text animation editing system designed to lower the barrier to entry for non-professional video creators. The system integrates inline edits and chat-based interactions, leveraging LLMs for intent understanding and creative suggestions. Key features include a semantic-animation mapping to translate user inputs into video editing actions, a dual-mode agent-based pipeline (Inline Agent and Chat Agent) for dynamic recommendations and natural language interactions, and a simplified workflow through bidirectional linking between script and timeline panels. A user study with 11 participants evaluated the system, demonstrating its usability and effectiveness in enabling AI-collaborative text animation creation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration of LLMs into a complete text animation editing workflow* beyond just content generation. The *dual-mode agent system* is a significant architectural contribution, allowing for context-aware inline suggestions and flexible conversational interactions. While other works have explored LLMs in video editing, the paper's specific focus on text animation with a dual agent approach is relatively novel. The semantic-animation mapping approach adds to the novelty, allowing the system to bridge the gap between textual intention and visual output. This integration distinguishes the work from tools primarily focused on automatic generation or summarization.
*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a real and increasingly important challenge: the difficulty of creating engaging video content for non-professionals. By simplifying the text animation workflow and empowering users with AI assistance, the system has the potential to significantly broaden participation in video creation. The user study results provide initial evidence of the system's effectiveness and usability. The work contributes significantly towards HCI for AI-assisted creative tools, providing valuable design principles for future systems. The detailed description of the system architecture, particularly the agent design and state monitoring, provides a solid foundation for others to build upon.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined problem and clear goals.
    *   Strong focus on usability and user experience.
    *   Novel dual-agent system architecture.
    *   Semantic-animation mapping bridge textual intent with visual output.
    *   Comprehensive user study demonstrating system effectiveness.
    *   Detailed explanation of the system's implementation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The limitations section acknowledges the time-sensitive nature of LLM research, implying the specific LLM implementations used might become outdated quickly. The paper would be stronger if it discussed broader architectural choices to mitigate this issue.
    *   The user study, while informative, has a relatively small sample size. A larger study would enhance the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The current system's capabilities are limited, and the paper acknowledges the need for improved agent architectures and multimodal capabilities.
    *   The reliance on external LLM APIs (like OpenAI's GPT-4) adds an element of dependence. Addressing this dependence through offline or more robust architectures would be a substantial improvement.
    *  Reliance on preset animations is restrictive and limiting, detracting from the paper's emphasis on precise editing.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can potentially impact the design of future AI-assisted video editing tools and other creative applications. The dual-agent architecture and semantic mapping approach offer a blueprint for building more intuitive and powerful systems. The design implications based on the user study results provide actionable guidance for researchers and developers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of AI-assisted video editing by introducing a practical and usable system for text animation creation. It combines several novel elements, including the dual-agent architecture, semantic-animation mapping, and the emphasis on user experience. Although it has some limitations, the paper provides a clear roadmap for future research and development in this area. The implementation details are comprehensive and can guide other researchers interested in building similar systems. I think this is a well-written and thorough paper, but the reliance on predefined animation is a serious drawback.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### OPT-BENCH: Evaluating LLM Agent on Large-Scale Search Spaces Optimization Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10764v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaozhe Li, Jixuan Chen, Xinyu Fang, Shengyuan Ding, Haodong Duan, Qingwen Liu, Kai Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in solving diverse tasks. However, their proficiency in iteratively optimizing complex solutions through learning from previous feedback remains insufficiently explored. To bridge this gap, we present OPT-BENCH, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate LLM agents on large-scale search space optimization problems. OPT-BENCH includes 20 real-world machine learning tasks sourced from Kaggle and 10 classical NP problems, offering a diverse and challenging environment for assessing LLM agents on iterative reasoning and solution refinement. To enable rigorous evaluation, we introduce OPT-Agent, an end-to-end optimization framework that emulates human reasoning when tackling complex problems by generating, validating, and iteratively improving solutions through leveraging historical feedback. Through extensive experiments on 9 state-of-the-art LLMs from 6 model families, we analyze the effects of optimization iterations, temperature settings, and model architectures on solution quality and convergence. Our results demonstrate that incorporating historical context significantly enhances optimization performance across both ML and NP tasks. All datasets, code, and evaluation tools are open-sourced to promote further research in advancing LLM-driven optimization and iterative reasoning. Project page: \href{https://github.com/OliverLeeXZ/OPT-BENCH}{https://github.com/OliverLeeXZ/OPT-BENCH}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OPT-BENCH, a new benchmark for evaluating the ability of Large Language Model (LLM) agents to solve large-scale search space optimization problems.  The benchmark consists of 20 real-world machine learning tasks from Kaggle and 10 classical NP-complete problems. To facilitate evaluation, the authors also propose OPT-Agent, an end-to-end optimization framework that mimics human reasoning by generating, validating, and iteratively improving solutions based on historical feedback. The paper presents experimental results using nine LLMs from six model families, analyzing the impact of various factors like optimization iterations, temperature settings, and model architectures. The results highlight the benefits of incorporating historical context and demonstrate that iterative refinement improves solution quality.  The benchmark and framework are open-sourced.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While LLMs have been evaluated on a variety of tasks, the focus on *iterative optimization within large search spaces, particularly using a mix of real-world ML tasks and NP-complete problems*, is a significant contribution. Many existing benchmarks rely on single-pass evaluations or synthetic tasks. The OPT-Agent framework, which aims to emulate human-like iterative refinement, is also a valuable addition. This iterative approach contrasts with prior single-pass evaluation of LLMs, which is a clear step forward. The inclusion of NP-complete problems, which are notoriously difficult to solve algorithmically, pushes the boundaries of what LLMs are typically evaluated on.
*   **Significance:** The benchmark fills a crucial gap in LLM evaluation by addressing the ability to learn from experience and refine solutions over multiple iterations. This is vital for real-world problem-solving where solutions are rarely perfect on the first attempt. The comprehensive experimental analysis offers insights into the factors influencing LLM optimization performance. The open-source nature of OPT-BENCH and OPT-Agent will encourage further research in this area, potentially leading to improvements in LLM-driven optimization techniques and iterative reasoning. The results highlight areas for improvement, particularly for open-source models on NP-complete problems. The thorough analysis of the effects of historical context, temperature, and draft vs. refine settings offers valuable practical guidance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The benchmark is comprehensive, diverse (ML and NP problems), and based on real-world tasks.
    *   The OPT-Agent framework provides a structured and automated way to evaluate iterative optimization.
    *   The experimental analysis is thorough, covering multiple LLMs and a range of hyperparameters.
    *   The paper provides clear insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different LLMs in optimization tasks.
    *   The open-source release facilitates further research and development.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The benchmark relies on Kaggle competitions, which might introduce biases related to data availability and problem framing.
    *   While the NP-complete problems are challenging, the specific instances chosen might not fully represent the difficulty of those problems in general. It would strengthen the paper if it described and justifies the instances of the NP problems chosen.
    *   While the case studies are informative, further detailed analysis would be benificial, particulary analysing the effectiveness of using historical info in relation to NP problems.
    *   The ML tasks tend to be in tabular data, not as varied in types of inputs compared to broader range of tasks LLMs are often used in.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution by introducing a novel and comprehensive benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on iterative optimization tasks. The combination of real-world ML tasks and classical NP problems, along with the OPT-Agent framework, represents a significant advance over existing evaluation methodologies. The experimental results offer valuable insights and practical guidance for improving LLM-driven optimization. While there are some limitations related to data biases and instance representation, the overall impact and potential influence of OPT-BENCH and OPT-Agent justify a high score. This is not a perfect score because some areas, such as a more exhaustive coverage of NP-complete problem instances and more variance in types of ML tasks considered, could be improved and expanded. Overall the papers importance in pushing the boundaries of LLM evaluation by emphasizing iterative refinement warrants a solid score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### One Tokenizer To Rule Them All: Emergent Language Plasticity via Multilingual Tokenizers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10766v1)
- **Authors**: Diana Abagyan, Alejandro R. Salamanca, Andres Felipe Cruz-Salinas, Kris Cao, Hangyu Lin, Acyr Locatelli, Marzieh Fadaee, Ahmet Üstün, Sara Hooker
- **Abstract**: Pretraining massively multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) for many languages at once is challenging due to limited model capacity, scarce high-quality data, and compute constraints. Moreover, the lack of language coverage of the tokenizer makes it harder to address the gap for new languages purely at the post-training stage. In this work, we study what relatively cheap interventions early on in training improve "language plasticity", or adaptation capabilities of the model post-training to new languages. We focus on tokenizer design and propose using a universal tokenizer that is trained for more languages than the primary pretraining languages to enable efficient adaptation in expanding language coverage after pretraining. Our systematic experiments across diverse groups of languages and different training strategies show that a universal tokenizer enables significantly higher language adaptation, with up to 20.2% increase in win rates compared to tokenizers specific to pretraining languages. Furthermore, a universal tokenizer also leads to better plasticity towards languages that are completely unseen in the tokenizer and pretraining, by up to 5% win rate gain. We achieve this adaptation to an expanded set of languages with minimal compromise in performance on the majority of languages included in pretraining.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how tokenizer design can improve the "language plasticity" of massively multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in adapting to new languages after pre-training. The authors propose using a "universal tokenizer" trained on a wider range of languages than the primary pre-training languages to enable more efficient adaptation. They conduct experiments across diverse language groups and training strategies, showing that a universal tokenizer leads to significantly higher language adaptation capabilities (up to 20.2% increase in win rates) compared to tokenizers specific to pre-training languages.  The universal tokenizer also improves plasticity towards completely unseen languages, and does so with minimal compromise on performance on pre-training languages. The paper concludes that this approach provides a cheap intervention early in training with potential for large downstream gains in language coverage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a universal tokenizer isn't entirely new, as multilingual models have always required some kind of tokenizer capable of handling multiple languages. However, this paper is novel in its focus on the *early* integration of a broader tokenizer during *pre-training* specifically to boost *post-training* adaptation, and in its systematic ablation study that demonstrates its effectiveness. Previous work often focuses on vocabulary extension or embedding layer adaptation *after* pre-training. The investigation of plasticity and targeted evaluation on unseen languages is also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a practical problem: the lack of language coverage in many state-of-the-art LLMs and the difficulty of extending this coverage post-training. A cheap, effective method for improving language adaptation could have a significant impact on making LLMs more accessible and useful for a wider range of languages and users. This is especially important for low-resource languages. By demonstrating how a subtle change in pre-training (tokenizer design) can yield meaningful downstream benefits, the paper provides valuable insights for practitioners and researchers. However, the actual effect of the win rates on downstream task is to be determined.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Experiments:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental setup, including extensive ablations on language subsets, adaptation strategies, and tokenizer variants across 69 languages.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of multilingual LLM development, specifically the limitations of pre-training coverage and the need for efficient adaptation methods.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The proposed approach is relatively simple and cost-effective, making it potentially appealing to practitioners with limited resources.
    *   **Robustness on Evaluation:** The use of both an LLM-as-judge (Command-A) on the evaluation side makes the comparison relatively reliable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the 20% gains are significant, some might argue that the underlying idea is an optimization rather than a paradigm shift. Whether this improvement results in tangible downstream improvement on zero-shot evaluation is to be further determined.
    *   **Vocabulary size trade-off:** The improved performance comes at the cost of an increased vocabulary size, which is to be considered in resource constraints.
    *   **Scope of the Limitation Section:** While the limitation section mentions model size, the current scale might not be representative of the performance of the most advanced LLMs, meaning future research with larger language model is essential.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future multilingual LLMs by highlighting the importance of tokenizer design in enabling efficient and effective language adaptation. It could also motivate further research into other pre-training interventions that improve plasticity and reduce the resource requirements for expanding language coverage.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel application of tokenizer design to improve language adaptation in multilingual LLMs. The systematic experiments, and practical implications, give the paper its value. However, the incremental nature of the improvement, the need for a larger vocabulary and that the overall scale of the paper limits its ultimate impact. The potential for practical adoption elevates its relevance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Different Questions, Different Models: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Uncertainty and Calibration in Clinical QA with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10769v1)
- **Authors**: Alberto Testoni, Iacer Calixto
- **Abstract**: Accurate and well-calibrated uncertainty estimates are essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) in high-stakes domains such as clinical decision support. We present a fine-grained evaluation of uncertainty estimation methods for clinical multiple-choice question answering, covering ten open-source LLMs (general-purpose, biomedical, and reasoning models) across two datasets, eleven medical specialties, and six question types. We compare standard single-generation and sampling-based methods, and present a case study exploring simple, single-pass estimators based on behavioral signals in reasoning traces. These lightweight methods approach the performance of Semantic Entropy while requiring only one generation. Our results reveal substantial variation across specialties and question types, underscoring the importance of selecting models based on both the nature of the question and model-specific strengths.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Different Questions, Different Models: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Uncertainty and Calibration in Clinical QA with LLMs" addresses a crucial gap in the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to clinical decision support: the lack of fine-grained evaluation of uncertainty quantification (UQ). The authors conduct a comprehensive evaluation of UQ methods across various LLMs (general-purpose, biomedical, and reasoning models), two datasets, eleven medical specialties, and six question types. They compare single-generation and sampling-based UQ techniques and introduce a novel approach leveraging behavioral signals from LLM reasoning traces as lightweight uncertainty proxies. The results highlight substantial performance variation across specialties and question types, emphasizing the need for context-aware model selection and the potential of reasoning traces for efficient UQ.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **breadth and granularity of evaluation**. While previous studies have explored UQ in clinical NLP, they have often been limited in the number of models, datasets, specialties, or question types considered.  The introduction of a new question type annotation is incremental, but its integration into a broader evaluation landscape provides significant value. The proposed method based on behavioral signals from reasoning traces also demonstrates potential. However, its novelty is somewhat limited since it builds upon existing observations about the nature of reasoning traces and only implements fairly straightforward summarization. While there's existing research on extracting value from model behavior as proxies for uncertainty, the authors present a novel combination of model behavior to reach results close to the ones achieved by Semantic Entropy.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is substantial, stemming from the critical need for reliable UQ in high-stakes clinical applications. Demonstrating how UQ performance varies significantly across different clinical contexts and question types is very important. This suggests that a single "one-size-fits-all" LLM solution may be inappropriate and highlights the importance of a nuanced, context-aware approach. The development of efficient UQ methods using reasoning traces is also valuable, as it addresses the computational cost associated with sampling-based techniques.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper's greatest strength is its scope. It systematically examines UQ across multiple dimensions (models, data, specialties, question types).
    *   **Practical Implications:** The results provide actionable guidance for selecting appropriate LLMs for specific clinical tasks and highlight areas where UQ methods need improvement.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The evaluation metrics are well-defined, and the experimental setup is sound.
    *   **Relevant Problem Addressed:** The paper focuses on UQ, which is paramount for responsible LLM deployment in clinical settings.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The results showing a mismatch between accuracy and calibration for certain models and question types are particularly insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Coverage:** While the number of models is reasonable, it's important to acknowledge that the landscape of LLMs is rapidly evolving. Some newer models or proprietary models are not included.
    *   **Feature Space Simplicity:** The features derived from reasoning traces are relatively basic and could be expanded to include more sophisticated linguistic or semantic information.
    *   **Limited Generalization of Features from Reasoning Traces:** It is a case study using only two reasoning LLMs, therefore the value added is difficult to generalize.
    *   **Incremental improvements:** While important, most metrics are only marginally increased.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in clinical NLP by motivating more granular evaluations of LLMs and inspiring the development of more context-aware UQ methods. The exploration of reasoning traces as a source of UQ signals also represents a promising direction.

**Overall:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by providing a comprehensive, fine-grained analysis of UQ in clinical question answering. It sheds light on the limitations of current LLMs and UQ methods and provides practical guidance for responsible deployment. While the novelty of individual components may be limited, the synthesis of different evaluation dimensions and the proposed UQ approach make the paper a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper excels in its comprehensive evaluation, addressing a critical need for robust UQ in clinical LLMs. It provides actionable insights for practitioners and motivates future research directions. While the feature design from reasoning traces could be more sophisticated, and coverage of less LLMs limits its applicability, the paper's breadth, rigor, and practical implications justify a score of 7.5. This score acknowledges its importance while also recognizing the potential for future work to build upon its findings with more advanced models, features, and UQ techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ME: Trigger Element Combination Backdoor Attack on Copyright Infringement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10776v1)
- **Authors**: Feiyu Yang, Siyuan Liang, Aishan Liu, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The capability of generative diffusion models (DMs) like Stable Diffusion (SD) in replicating training data could be taken advantage of by attackers to launch the Copyright Infringement Attack, with duplicated poisoned image-text pairs. SilentBadDiffusion (SBD) is a method proposed recently, which shew outstanding performance in attacking SD in text-to-image tasks. However, the feasible data resources in this area are still limited, some of them are even constrained or prohibited due to the issues like copyright ownership or inappropriate contents; And not all of the images in current datasets are suitable for the proposed attacking methods; Besides, the state-of-the-art (SoTA) performance of SBD is far from ideal when few generated poisoning samples could be adopted for attacks. In this paper, we raised new datasets accessible for researching in attacks like SBD, and proposed Multi-Element (ME) attack method based on SBD by increasing the number of poisonous visual-text elements per poisoned sample to enhance the ability of attacking, while importing Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) for the poisoned samples to maintain the stealthiness. The Copyright Infringement Rate (CIR) / First Attack Epoch (FAE) we got on the two new datasets were 16.78% / 39.50 and 51.20% / 23.60, respectively close to or even outperformed benchmark Pokemon and Mijourney datasets. In condition of low subsampling ratio (5%, 6 poisoned samples), MESI and DCT earned CIR / FAE of 0.23% / 84.00 and 12.73% / 65.50, both better than original SBD, which failed to attack at all.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ME: Trigger Element Combination Backdoor Attack on Copyright Infringement":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the copyright infringement vulnerability of diffusion models like Stable Diffusion (SD).  It focuses on improving the SilentBadDiffusion (SBD) attack, which inserts poisoned image-text pairs into the training dataset.  The authors argue that existing data resources for researching such attacks are limited and that SBD's performance is suboptimal with few poisoned samples. They propose two main contributions:

1.  **New Datasets:** They introduce two new datasets, Style and DiffusionDB, for studying copyright infringement attacks.
2.  **Modified Attacking Methods:**  They present Multi-Element (ME) and ME combined with Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) attacks. ME increases the number of visual-text trigger elements in each poisoned sample, while DCT is used to make these samples more stealthy by reducing visual fidelity. They show that their methods achieve better copyright infringement rates (CIR) and first attack epochs (FAE) compared to the original SBD, especially when only a small number of poisoned samples are used.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Datasets:** The introduction of Style and DiffusionDB addresses a legitimate concern about the limited availability and potential legal issues associated with existing datasets for copyright infringement attack research.  However, DiffusionDB already exists, although perhaps not specifically curated for this purpose. The creation of Style, while synthesized, can still offer a means to evaluate various attack methodologies where direct visual copyright of data is non-existent. The contribution here is moderate.
*   **ME Attack:** The concept of increasing the number of trigger elements per poisoned sample to enhance the attack is conceptually straightforward, but its demonstration and analysis of its effectiveness, particularly under low subsampling conditions, constitutes some novelty.
*   **DCT Integration:** The use of DCT to improve stealthiness is a reasonable approach, as it reduces the visual similarity between poisoned samples and target images. While DCT itself is not new, its application in this context for SBD is novel.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a real and growing concern about the potential for diffusion models to be exploited for copyright infringement. The improvements to SBD, particularly under low subsampling conditions, are relevant as they reflect more realistic attack scenarios.
*   **Impact on the Field:** The paper offers a concrete and actionable methodology to improve copyright infringement attacks. The new datasets provide a valuable resource for future research. Additionally, highlighting the tradeoffs between trigger element density and visual stealthiness is an important contribution to the field.
*   **Weakness:** The paper, like other SBD based work, does not consider defensive measures which can be taken. Nor does it provide more complex attack approaches, such as those which would use the model being poisoned to increase the effectiveness of the poisoning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a tangible improvement on an existing attack methodology and provides new resources for future research in a relevant and rapidly evolving field. The combination of multiple trigger elements and DCT for stealthiness is a clever approach that yields better results, especially in challenging low-subsampling scenarios.  However, the individual components (ME and DCT) are relatively simple.

I am assigning a score of 7/10. The new datasets and improved attack methods provide a solid, incremental contribution to the field. However, the core ideas are not groundbreaking, and the paper could benefit from a deeper exploration of the trade-offs and limitations of the proposed techniques, and consideration of existing defensive measures or more involved attacks. The empirical evaluation, while comprehensive, could also explore a wider range of parameter settings and dataset combinations, as well as more modern attacks on dataset privacy.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### What Users Value and Critique: Large-Scale Analysis of User Feedback on AI-Powered Mobile Apps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10785v1)
- **Authors**: Vinaik Chhetri, Krishna Upadhyay, A. B. Siddique, Umar Farooq
- **Abstract**: Artificial Intelligence (AI)-powered features have rapidly proliferated across mobile apps in various domains, including productivity, education, entertainment, and creativity. However, how users perceive, evaluate, and critique these AI features remains largely unexplored, primarily due to the overwhelming volume of user feedback. In this work, we present the first comprehensive, large-scale study of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps, leveraging a curated dataset of 292 AI-driven apps across 14 categories with 894K AI-specific reviews from Google Play. We develop and validate a multi-stage analysis pipeline that begins with a human-labeled benchmark and systematically evaluates large language models (LLMs) and prompting strategies. Each stage, including review classification, aspect-sentiment extraction, and clustering, is validated for accuracy and consistency. Our pipeline enables scalable, high-precision analysis of user feedback, extracting over one million aspect-sentiment pairs clustered into 18 positive and 15 negative user topics. Our analysis reveals that users consistently focus on a narrow set of themes: positive comments emphasize productivity, reliability, and personalized assistance, while negative feedback highlights technical failures (e.g., scanning and recognition), pricing concerns, and limitations in language support. Our pipeline surfaces both satisfaction with one feature and frustration with another within the same review. These fine-grained, co-occurring sentiments are often missed by traditional approaches that treat positive and negative feedback in isolation or rely on coarse-grained analysis. To this end, our approach provides a more faithful reflection of the real-world user experiences with AI-powered apps. Category-aware analysis further uncovers both universal drivers of satisfaction and domain-specific frustrations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a large-scale analysis of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps. It leverages a curated dataset of 894K reviews from 292 apps across 14 categories. The authors develop a multi-stage analysis pipeline that uses LLMs for review classification, aspect-sentiment extraction, and clustering. The analysis identifies key user concerns and valued features, reveals co-occurring sentiments within reviews, and explores variations across app categories. The study uncovers that users value productivity, reliability, and personalized assistance, while common complaints relate to technical failures, pricing, and language support.  The category-aware analysis reveals both universal trends and domain-specific expectations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its *scale and scope*. While previous work has examined user reviews of mobile apps and AI-related topics separately, this study is the first comprehensive effort to analyze user feedback specifically on AI-powered features across a diverse range of mobile app categories. The authors also present a well-defined and validated LLM-based pipeline for this task.  The extraction and clustering of aspect-sentiment pairs to identify fine-grained topics is a valuable methodological contribution.

*   **Significance:** The study provides valuable insights for software engineers and app developers, offering actionable guidance for aligning AI features with user expectations. Identifying frequently co-occurring positive and negative sentiments is particularly useful for prioritizing improvements.  The category-aware analysis allows for more targeted development efforts. The public release of the dataset and pipeline code fosters further research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-scale dataset:** The size and diversity of the dataset are significant assets.
    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The authors carefully validate each stage of their analysis pipeline.
    *   **Actionable insights:** The findings offer concrete recommendations for improving AI-powered apps.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The public release of data and code promotes replication and extension.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The analysis depends heavily on the performance of LLMs, which are known to have limitations (e.g., bias, hallucination). Although the authors attempt to mitigate these by using the best available LLM and validation steps, the results could be influenced by these inherent LLM characteristics. The prompts may have also introduced some bias.
    *   **Google Play Store Bias:** The reliance on Google Play Store data limits the generalizability of the findings to other app platforms (e.g., Apple's App Store), which may have different user demographics and review cultures.
    *   **Definition of "AI-powered":** The reliance on explicit mentions of AI in app descriptions might exclude some apps that implicitly leverage AI or use it in the backend.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While comprehensive, the analysis remains somewhat descriptive. A more in-depth qualitative analysis of specific examples or cases might uncover richer insights.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the following:

    *   **AI-powered mobile app development:** Developers can use the findings to prioritize features and address user concerns.
    *   **User experience research:** The methodology can be adapted to study user feedback on other AI-related technologies.
    *   **NLP and machine learning:** The dataset and pipeline can be used to develop and evaluate new methods for analyzing user reviews.

**Overall Assessment:**

Despite the weaknesses related to the inherent limitations of LLMs and dataset scope, the paper represents a significant contribution due to its large-scale analysis, rigorous methodology, and actionable insights. The study addresses a critical gap in understanding user perceptions of AI-powered features in mobile apps and provides valuable guidance for developers and researchers. Given the scale and systematic approach, the insights are both practically valuable and scientifically sound.

### FASCIST-O-METER: Classifier for Neo-fascist Discourse Online
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10789v1)
- **Authors**: Rudy Alexandro Garrido Veliz, Martin Semmann, Chris Biemann, Seid Muhie Yimam
- **Abstract**: Neo-fascism is a political and societal ideology that has been having remarkable growth in the last decade in the United States of America (USA), as well as in other Western societies. It poses a grave danger to democracy and the minorities it targets, and it requires active actions against it to avoid escalation. This work presents the first-of-its-kind neo-fascist coding scheme for digital discourse in the USA societal context, overseen by political science researchers. Our work bridges the gap between Natural Language Processing (NLP) and political science against this phenomena. Furthermore, to test the coding scheme, we collect a tremendous amount of activity on the internet from notable neo-fascist groups (the forums of Iron March and Stormfront.org), and the guidelines are applied to a subset of the collected posts. Through crowdsourcing, we annotate a total of a thousand posts that are labeled as neo-fascist or non-neo-fascist. With this labeled data set, we fine-tune and test both Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs), obtaining the very first classification models for neo-fascist discourse. We find that the prevalence of neo-fascist rhetoric in this kind of forum is ever-present, making them a good target for future research. The societal context is a key consideration for neo-fascist speech when conducting NLP research. Finally, the work against this kind of political movement must be pressed upon and continued for the well-being of a democratic society. Disclaimer: This study focuses on detecting neo-fascist content in text, similar to other hate speech analyses, without labeling individuals or organizations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "FASCIST-O-METER," a system designed to classify neo-fascist discourse online, specifically within the USA societal context. It addresses the growing concern of neo-fascism and its threat to democracy. The authors collected data from known neo-fascist forums (Iron March and Stormfront.org), developed annotation guidelines with input from political science experts, and crowdsourced annotations to create a labeled dataset. This dataset was then used to train and test both Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs) for neo-fascist discourse detection. The study explores the challenges in annotating such content, evaluates the performance of various language models, and aims to contribute to future research and mitigation efforts against the spread of neo-fascism online.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to present the first-of-its-kind neo-fascist coding scheme for digital discourse. This is a significant claim. While there is existing research on hate speech, extremism, and political bias detection, a dedicated effort focused explicitly on *neo-fascist discourse*, specifically considering the societal context of the USA, appears to be a novel contribution. The annotated dataset is also a valuable asset that the authors have made publicly available, encouraging further research in the field. The models can serve as a baseline for follow up studies.

*   **Significance:** Neo-fascism's potential threat to democratic societies and minorities makes research in this area vital. By bridging the gap between NLP and political science, the paper tackles a complex issue with a multidisciplinary approach. The development of a classifier capable of identifying neo-fascist discourse can assist in detecting and potentially mitigating the spread of such ideologies online. This is particularly important given the potential for online radicalization and real-world consequences. The explicit focus on U.S. societal context also strengthens this significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Interdisciplinary Approach:** The collaboration between NLP researchers and political science experts strengthens the study's foundation and ensures the annotation guidelines and coding scheme are theoretically sound and contextually relevant.
    *   **Data Collection & Annotation:** The collection of data from known neo-fascist forums provides a valuable resource for research. The detailed annotation guidelines, created with expert input, contribute to the reliability and validity of the labeled dataset.
    *   **Model Evaluation:** The evaluation of various SLMs and LLMs offers insights into the suitability of different models for classifying neo-fascist discourse, providing a starting point for future research in model selection and optimization.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper explicitly addresses ethical considerations, including the potentially triggering effect of the content and the importance of anonymizing data and avoiding the classification of individuals based on political beliefs.
    *   **Dataset availability**: Encouraging.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The annotated dataset, while valuable, consists of only 1000 entries. This relatively small size might limit the generalizability of the trained classifiers and potentially hinder the ability of the models to capture the full spectrum of neo-fascist discourse.
    *   **LLM performance**: LLMs, outside of GPT-40, show extremely poor performance in this study. While some can be related to the lack of uncensored models being trained, this is a limitation of the study.
    *   **Lack of real-world deployment or mitigation strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on identifying and classifying neo-fascist discourse but does not explore how this information can be used for practical interventions or mitigation strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The FASCIST-O-METER can serve as a foundational tool for future research in this area. The annotated dataset provides a benchmark for evaluating other methods. The identified challenges in annotating neo-fascist content and the performance of different language models can guide future research directions. The tool and the research can potentially inform the development of content moderation strategies, educational initiatives, and counter-narratives aimed at combating neo-fascism online.

*   **Justification of Score:**
    While the paper demonstrates novelty, its limited dataset and primary focus on classification without real-world mitigation strategies warrant a less transformative score. The creation of the novel dataset and the interdisciplinary angle adds to the significance, but the small size dataset hinders its effectiveness.

### Mitigating Negative Interference in Multilingual Sequential Knowledge Editing through Null-Space Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10800v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Sun, Tingyu Qu, Mingxiao Li, Jesse Davis, Marie-Francine Moens
- **Abstract**: Efficiently updating multilingual knowledge in large language models (LLMs), while preserving consistent factual representations across languages, remains a long-standing and unresolved challenge. While deploying separate editing systems for each language might seem viable, this approach incurs substantial costs due to the need to manage multiple models. A more efficient solution involves integrating knowledge updates across all languages into a unified model. However, performing sequential edits across languages often leads to destructive parameter interference, significantly degrading multilingual generalization and the accuracy of injected knowledge. To address this challenge, we propose LangEdit, a novel null-space constrained framework designed to precisely isolate language-specific knowledge updates. The core innovation of LangEdit lies in its ability to project parameter updates for each language onto the orthogonal complement of previous updated subspaces. This approach mathematically guarantees update independence while preserving multilingual generalization capabilities. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation across three model architectures, six languages, and four downstream tasks, demonstrating that LangEdit effectively mitigates parameter interference and outperforms existing state-of-the-art editing methods. Our results highlight its potential for enabling efficient and accurate multilingual knowledge updates in LLMs. The code is available at https://github.com/VRCMF/LangEdit.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LangEdit, a novel framework designed to mitigate negative interference in multilingual sequential knowledge editing of large language models (LLMs).  Multilingual sequential knowledge editing is the task of updating knowledge across multiple languages in a sequential manner. A key challenge is that edits in one language can negatively impact performance in other languages (negative interference). LangEdit addresses this by constraining parameter updates for each language to lie in the null space of previously updated language subspaces. This mathematically guarantees update independence and preserves multilingual generalization.  The authors perform extensive experiments across multiple model architectures, languages, and downstream tasks, demonstrating that LangEdit outperforms existing state-of-the-art editing methods in terms of efficacy, generality, and specificity, while preserving multilingual generalization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its proposal of a null-space constrained framework (LangEdit) for multilingual sequential knowledge editing.  While null-space projection techniques have been used in continual learning (specifically for computer vision), the application to *multilingual knowledge editing* is novel.  The paper also introduces the *task* of multilingual sequential knowledge editing which in itself is a worthwhile contribution. The idea of actively preventing interference between languages during sequential editing by mathematically decoupling the updates is a strong and intuitively appealing approach.  The incremental update of covariance matrices for computational efficiency is also a useful practical contribution. It seems like the problem addressed and the solution proposed are technically sound.

* **Significance:**  The ability to efficiently and accurately update knowledge in multilingual LLMs is crucial for real-world applications. By addressing the problem of negative interference, LangEdit offers a significant improvement over existing methods. The extensive experimental results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of LangEdit across a diverse set of models, languages, and tasks. The improvement in multilingual generalization is particularly important. The paper takes a methodical approach, comparing to multiple baselines, and justifying the methodology.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Formulation:** Clearly defines the problem of negative interference in multilingual sequential knowledge editing.
    * **Methodology:** LangEdit is mathematically well-founded and intuitively understandable.  The use of null-space projection provides a strong theoretical basis.
    * **Experimental Evaluation:** Comprehensive experiments across diverse models, languages, and downstream tasks, providing strong empirical evidence for the effectiveness of LangEdit.
    * **Performance:**  Significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art editing methods.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges that computing the null space can be computationally expensive, although they try to address it through covariance matrices. More work could be done to scale the proposed approach to larger models and knowledge bases.
    * **Limited Language Coverage:** While the experiments cover several languages, it is important to consider whether the results generalize to a broader range of languages, especially low-resource languages. The conclusion about larger models could also be supported by some experiments.
    * **Null Space Approximation**: The method uses approximate null-space calculation. While the paper justifies it for efficiency, a deeper analysis of the approximation's impact on update independence and overall performance would be beneficial.

* **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field of knowledge editing by providing a principled and effective approach for multilingual knowledge updates.  It addresses a challenging problem with a clear and well-validated solution, and it provides a strong foundation for future research in this area.  The introduction of the task itself will also prompt future research.

**Justification of Score:**

LangEdit provides a solid contribution to the field, solving a real problem and demonstrating good improvement. While it builds upon existing null-space techniques, its application to the task is novel and shows promising results. The identified weaknesses regarding computational scalability and language coverage are potential areas for future research. Taking into consideration the solid theoretical grounding, convincing empirical results, and the potential for influencing future research, the paper deserves a good score.
