### The Diffusion Duality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10892v1)
- **Authors**: Subham Sekhar Sahoo, Justin Deschenaux, Aaron Gokaslan, Guanghan Wang, Justin Chiu, Volodymyr Kuleshov
- **Abstract**: Uniform-state discrete diffusion models hold the promise of fast text generation due to their inherent ability to self-correct. However, they are typically outperformed by autoregressive models and masked diffusion models. In this work, we narrow this performance gap by leveraging a key insight: Uniform-state diffusion processes naturally emerge from an underlying Gaussian diffusion. Our method, Duo, transfers powerful techniques from Gaussian diffusion to improve both training and sampling. First, we introduce a curriculum learning strategy guided by the Gaussian process, doubling training speed by reducing variance. Models trained with curriculum learning surpass autoregressive models in zero-shot perplexity on 3 of 7 benchmarks. Second, we present Discrete Consistency Distillation, which adapts consistency distillation from the continuous to the discrete setting. This algorithm unlocks few-step generation in diffusion language models by accelerating sampling by two orders of magnitude. We provide the code and model checkpoints on the project page: http://s-sahoo.github.io/duo
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper, "The Diffusion Duality":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Duo, a framework that leverages the connection between Uniform-state Discrete Diffusion Models (USDMs) and underlying Gaussian diffusion processes. The key insight is that USDMs, often used for text generation, can be viewed as emerging from Gaussian diffusion. Duo uses this connection to improve USDM training and sampling in two main ways: (1) a curriculum learning strategy guided by the Gaussian process to reduce training variance and speed up training, and (2) a Discrete Consistency Distillation method adapted from continuous diffusion to accelerate sampling for faster, few-step generation. The paper claims improved performance compared to autoregressive models on some benchmarks and demonstrates a significant speedup in sampling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of linking discrete and continuous diffusion through the `argmax` operation is interesting and contributes a novel perspective to the field. Adapting techniques like curriculum learning and consistency distillation from the well-studied Gaussian diffusion domain to the less-developed discrete setting is a valuable direction.
*   **Significance:** Faster training and sampling are crucial for the practical adoption of diffusion models in text generation. Demonstrating these improvements with Duo, particularly in the few-step generation regime, addresses a significant limitation. The paper's code release and model checkpoints contribute to reproducibility and facilitate further research.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong theoretical grounding: The paper provides a theoretical justification for the connection between Gaussian and Uniform-state diffusion models.
    *   Empirical validation: The experiments show improvements in training speed, zero-shot perplexity, and sampling speed.
    *   Practical methods: The proposed curriculum learning and Discrete Consistency Distillation techniques are directly applicable to USDMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited competitive advantage: While the paper reports surpassing autoregressive models on some benchmarks, it still acknowledges that USDMs trail Masked Discrete Diffusion Models (MDMs) in terms of perplexity.  The performance gap between USDMs and MDMs isn't completely closed, suggesting the limitations of the framework.
    *   Dependency on Gaussian diffusion: The method is reliant on transferring techniques from Gaussian diffusion, which might limit the exploration of unique properties of discrete diffusion.
    *   Ablation clarity needed:  While there is some ablations regarding components of DUO, more fine-grained decomposition might be useful for the reader to understand the value of the methods.

*   **Potential influence:** By showing the link between discrete and continuous diffusion, this paper might inspire more hybrid approaches that leverage the strengths of both domains. The acceleration of USDM training and sampling could make them more competitive with other generative models for text.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable connection between continuous and discrete diffusion frameworks and demonstrates practical improvements to USDMs for text generation. While it has limitations, the combination of theoretical insight and empirical validation contributes positively to the field. However, the performance increases over established models may not be extremely significant, thus warranting a lower score.

- **Score**: 7/10

### GenPlanX. Generation of Plans and Execution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10897v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Borrajo, Giuseppe Canonaco, Tomás de la Rosa, Alfredo Garrachón, Sriram Gopalakrishnan, Simerjot Kaur, Marianela Morales, Sunandita Patra, Alberto Pozanco, Keshav Ramani, Charese Smiley, Pietro Totis, Manuela Veloso
- **Abstract**: Classical AI Planning techniques generate sequences of actions for complex tasks. However, they lack the ability to understand planning tasks when provided using natural language. The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has introduced novel capabilities in human-computer interaction. In the context of planning tasks, LLMs have shown to be particularly good in interpreting human intents among other uses. This paper introduces GenPlanX that integrates LLMs for natural language-based description of planning tasks, with a classical AI planning engine, alongside an execution and monitoring framework. We demonstrate the efficacy of GenPlanX in assisting users with office-related tasks, highlighting its potential to streamline workflows and enhance productivity through seamless human-AI collaboration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GenPlanX paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GenPlanX, a system that integrates Large Language Models (LLMs) with classical AI planning techniques for automating office-related tasks. GenPlanX takes natural language requests, uses an LLM to interpret the intent and generate a structured problem description, translates this description into PDDL (Planning Domain Definition Language), uses a classical planner to generate an executable plan, and then executes and monitors that plan. The system includes entity extraction to understand domain-specific information and handles replanning in case of failures. The paper demonstrates the system's effectiveness with examples related to data manipulation, presentation generation, and prediction model learning. It shows scenarios where GenPlanX generates optimal plans that LLMs alone might miss, highlighting the benefits of combining LLM understanding with the reliability and optimality guarantees of classical planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The novelty and significance of this paper lie in the integration of LLMs and classical AI planning within a practical, office-related task framework that seamlessly handles planning and execution. The system cleverly uses LLMs for what they're good at – understanding natural language and extracting intent – while leveraging the reliability and optimality guarantees of traditional AI planning for task execution. The modular architecture, with components like Entity Extraction, Prompt Generation, LLM, Compiler, Planner, and Execution, adds to the overall merit.

*Strengths:*

*   **Practical application**: Focus on automating real-world office tasks makes the research more applicable than purely theoretical explorations.
*   **Hybrid approach**: The combination of LLMs and classical planning addresses the limitations of LLMs (lack of guarantees of correctness or optimality) and classical planning (difficulty understanding natural language).
*   **Robustness**: Built-in error handling and replanning capabilities enhance the system's reliability in dynamic environments.
*   **Modularity:** The distinct modules (Entity Extraction, Prompt Generation, LLM, Compiler, Planner, Execution) offer flexibility and ease of maintenance/extension.
*   **Clear Demonstration:**  The examples and detailed explanations of the system components in operation showcase the strengths and potential of GenPlanX. The comparison with direct LLM planning highlights the value of the hybrid approach, as well.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Fixed Domain Model**: The reliance on a pre-defined domain model (expressed in PDDL) limits the system's adaptability to completely novel or unpredictable tasks. The paper doesn't offer much discussion on how easy or difficult it is to create or modify the PDDL models for new applications. The effort required to design/maintain the domain model could be significant, hindering scaling to a very large variety of tasks.
*   **LLM Prompt Engineering:** The paper discusses creating dynamic prompts. There might be challenges in designing prompts that generalize well across diverse user requests. While Entity Extraction helps, a poorly designed prompt could still lead to incorrect intent extraction and suboptimal planning.
*   **Monitoring Limitations**: The current monitoring system only tracks low-level state, without high-level PDDL state monitoring. This might limit the system's ability to detect and recover from more complex execution failures. The feedback loop isn't fully optimized.

**Significance and Potential Impact:**

GenPlanX has the potential to significantly impact the field of human-AI collaboration in office environments. By automating complex tasks, it can enhance productivity and streamline workflows. The modular architecture facilitates integrating new tools and applications, making the system adaptable to evolving needs. The concept of offloading intent understanding to LLMs while relying on classical planners for reliable plan generation is a valuable contribution to the field, addressing a critical challenge in real-world AI applications.

**Justification for the score:**

While GenPlanX exhibits strong practical relevance and a well-designed architecture, the dependence on a fixed domain model and the current limitations in execution monitoring prevent it from achieving a truly transformative impact. The paper shows a clear and valuable application of existing techniques (LLMs and classical planning) to create a useful system. However, it's not a fundamentally groundbreaking advance, and the fixed PDDL domain limits its scalability. Furthermore, a much more robust empirical evaluation (beyond just the examples) is needed to fully understand its effectiveness.

### Beyond Gold Standards: Epistemic Ensemble of LLM Judges for Formal Mathematical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10903v1)
- **Authors**: Lan Zhang, Marco Valentino, Andre Freitas
- **Abstract**: Autoformalization plays a crucial role in formal mathematical reasoning by enabling the automatic translation of natural language statements into formal languages. While recent advances using large language models (LLMs) have shown promising results, methods for automatically evaluating autoformalization remain underexplored. As one moves to more complex domains (e.g., advanced mathematics), human evaluation requires significant time and domain expertise, especially as the complexity of the underlying statements and background knowledge increases. LLM-as-a-judge presents a promising approach for automating such evaluation. However, existing methods typically employ coarse-grained and generic evaluation criteria, which limit their effectiveness for advanced formal mathematical reasoning, where quality hinges on nuanced, multi-granular dimensions. In this work, we take a step toward addressing this gap by introducing a systematic, automatic method to evaluate autoformalization tasks. The proposed method is based on an epistemically and formally grounded ensemble (EFG) of LLM judges, defined on criteria encompassing logical preservation (LP), mathematical consistency (MC), formal validity (FV), and formal quality (FQ), resulting in a transparent assessment that accounts for different contributing factors. We validate the proposed framework to serve as a proxy for autoformalization assessment within the domain of formal mathematics. Overall, our experiments demonstrate that the EFG ensemble of LLM judges is a suitable emerging proxy for evaluation, more strongly correlating with human assessments than a coarse-grained model, especially when assessing formal qualities. These findings suggest that LLM-as-judges, especially when guided by a well-defined set of atomic properties, could offer a scalable, interpretable, and reliable support for evaluating formal mathematical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond Gold Standards: Epistemic Ensemble of LLM Judges for Formal Mathematical Reasoning" addresses the challenge of evaluating autoformalization, the task of translating natural language mathematical statements into formal languages.  The authors argue that existing evaluation methods are limited, especially in complex mathematical domains, due to reliance on coarse-grained criteria and the need for significant human expertise. They propose a novel method based on an "Epistemic and Formally Grounded (EFG)" ensemble of LLM judges. This ensemble evaluates candidate formalizations across four key aspects: logical preservation, mathematical consistency, formal validity, and formal quality, using a taxonomy of atomic properties. LLMs are used to judge these properties, and their judgments are synthesized into an overall assessment score. The authors validate their framework by comparing its performance to human evaluations on existing datasets like miniF2F and ProofNet, demonstrating improved correlation with human judgments compared to traditional metrics like BLEU and ChrF. The paper also investigates the impact of different LLM sizes and prompting strategies on the evaluation process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a significant advancement in the evaluation of autoformalization.  The EFG framework is genuinely novel in its systematic and multi-faceted approach. Breaking down the evaluation into specific, epistemically grounded criteria like logical preservation and mathematical consistency allows for a more nuanced and interpretable assessment than simply relying on pass/fail metrics or reference-based methods. While the concept of using LLMs as judges isn't entirely new, the way this paper structures the judging process, with a clear taxonomy and operable atomic properties, is a substantial improvement over existing approaches, which often use general-purpose, poorly defined prompts. The use of a linear evaluation model to integrate the atomic properties is straightforward but effective in creating a unified assessment.

**Significance:** The paper's contribution lies in providing a more scalable and interpretable method for evaluating autoformalization.  The need for automated evaluation becomes critical as autoformalization models improve and tackle increasingly complex mathematical problems. Human evaluation is time-consuming, expensive, and requires specialized expertise, making it a significant bottleneck. The proposed framework mitigates this bottleneck by providing a reliable proxy for human evaluation. The interpretable nature of the evaluation (breaking it down into specific criteria) is also a major benefit, as it allows for targeted improvement of autoformalization models based on specific weaknesses. The demonstrated correlation with human judgment is a strong indicator of the framework's potential. While only tested on a limited set of datasets, the insights on prompt design and different performance among LLM families point the direction to further research and improve the evaluation approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Framework:** The EFG taxonomy and operable atomic properties provide a clear and structured approach to autoformalization evaluation.
*   **Scalability:** The framework enables automated evaluation, addressing the limitations of human-based assessment.
*   **Interpretability:** The multi-faceted evaluation provides insights into the strengths and weaknesses of autoformalization models.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper demonstrates strong correlation with human judgment, validating the framework's effectiveness.
*   **Analysis of LLM Judges:** The exploration of different LLM sizes and prompting strategies provides valuable guidance for using LLMs as evaluators.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Datasets:** The evaluation is primarily based on miniF2F and ProofNet.  Expanding the evaluation to a broader range of datasets, especially those containing more complex and novel mathematical concepts, would strengthen the findings.
*   **Linear Evaluation Model:** While effective, the linear evaluation model could be refined using more sophisticated weighting schemes (e.g., learned weights or non-linear combinations). The choice of weights also needs further justification.
*   **Dependency on LLMs:** The framework's reliability depends on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. As LLMs evolve, the framework may require adaptation and recalibration.
*   **Lack of fine-tuning on OAPs evaluation tasks:** A limited number of examples are provided in this specific area and no dedicated fine-tuning of LLMs as judges is provided.

**Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of autoformalization by providing a more effective and scalable evaluation methodology. The EFG framework can be adopted by researchers and practitioners to benchmark autoformalization models, identify areas for improvement, and develop more robust and reliable systems for translating mathematical statements into formal languages. The insights on using LLMs as judges can also inform future research on developing automated evaluation systems for other complex reasoning tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a significant advance, there are areas for further development that limits the impact. The limitations related to the linear weighting scheme and reliance of the judgements of LLMs affect the replicability of the findings. Furthermore, the lack of fine-tuning of the LLMs on this specific task constraints the benefits of the proposed approach.

### Probably Approximately Correct Labels
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10908v1)
- **Authors**: Emmanuel J. Candès, Andrew Ilyas, Tijana Zrnic
- **Abstract**: Obtaining high-quality labeled datasets is often costly, requiring either extensive human annotation or expensive experiments. We propose a method that supplements such "expert" labels with AI predictions from pre-trained models to construct labeled datasets more cost-effectively. Our approach results in probably approximately correct labels: with high probability, the overall labeling error is small. This solution enables rigorous yet efficient dataset curation using modern AI models. We demonstrate the benefits of the methodology through text annotation with large language models, image labeling with pre-trained vision models, and protein folding analysis with AlphaFold.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Probably Approximately Correct Labels":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a methodology called "Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) labeling" for creating labeled datasets more cost-effectively. The approach combines inexpensive labels from AI models with a limited number of expensive "expert" labels. The goal is to produce a dataset where, with high probability (1-α), the overall labeling error is below a specified threshold (ε). The method relies on uncertainty scores from the AI models to strategically focus expert labeling efforts on instances where the AI is most uncertain. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of PAC labeling on tasks across natural language processing, computer vision, and protein structure prediction. They explore variations including uncertainty calibration and multi-model routing to further improve the cost-effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining AI-predicted labels with selective expert labels to guarantee a certain level of accuracy is valuable and relevant. While related works exist on efficient dataset labeling and active learning, this paper distinguishes itself by (a) focusing on off-the-shelf pre-trained AI models, (b) avoiding complexity assumptions about the expert labeling mechanism, and (c) providing rigorous error guarantees. The idea of using a smooth approximation of the PAC condition to train a router between different labelers is also a new and interesting perspective. The connection to prediction-powered inference provides a solid theoretical grounding.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in machine learning: the high cost of creating labeled datasets. By providing a principled method for leveraging AI, it has the potential to significantly reduce annotation costs while maintaining data quality. This is especially important in high-stakes applications where reliable labels are crucial. The empirical results show promising cost savings across diverse domains. Furthermore, the extension to the multi-model setting makes the method very appealing in real-world conditions, where several annotators can give labels with different quality. The authors are clear on the assumptions and limitations, fostering trust in their results. The authors' willingness to provide the code also makes their results more accessible to the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem and provides a well-defined solution.
    *   **Theoretical Guarantees:** The PAC framework provides strong theoretical guarantees on labeling error.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The methodology is thoroughly evaluated on real datasets across multiple domains.
    *   **Practical Extensions:** The paper explores useful extensions such as uncertainty calibration and multi-model routing.
    *   **Clarity of Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on AI Model Quality:** The effectiveness of PAC labeling heavily depends on the quality of the pre-trained AI models. If the AI models are unreliable, the method might still require significant expert labeling. Although the router method alleviates this by combining several AI labelers, the expert labeler may still be required more than what's ideal.
    *   **Uncertainty Score Quality:** The method's performance also hinges on the quality of the uncertainty scores. A bad calibration of the uncertainty of the base model will result in a waste of the expert budget. Even the more refined method of uncertainty multicalibration still assumes that the uncertainty scores can be adjusted using a small subset of expert labels.
    *   **Scalability of multi-model setting:** The multi-model setting assumes one has access to a small, fully labelled routing dataset of size *m*. The practicality of the routing dataset is not tested, in cases in which obtaining those labels may be harder than a simple label.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could have a significant influence on how datasets are created and curated. It could enable the development of more efficient and cost-effective labeling pipelines. The ideas presented in this paper can be used in many data settings. Moreover, the theoretical guarantees allow it to be employed with confidence in downstream tasks.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a valuable and practically relevant method for creating labeled datasets with guarantees on accuracy. The theoretical grounding, empirical validation, and exploration of extensions add to the paper's significance. While there are some limitations regarding the dependence on AI model and uncertainty score quality, the potential benefits of PAC labeling are substantial. The paper is well-written and technically sound, and the authors have clearly outlined the scope and limitations of their approach. Therefore, while not revolutionary, the paper constitutes a solid contribution with the potential for significant impact within the field. The score reflects the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses within its domain.

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### NoLoCo: No-all-reduce Low Communication Training Method for Large Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10911v1)
- **Authors**: Jari Kolehmainen, Nikolay Blagoev, John Donaghy, Oğuzhan Ersoy, Christopher Nies
- **Abstract**: Training large language models is generally done via optimization methods on clusters containing tens of thousands of accelerators, communicating over a high-bandwidth interconnect. Scaling up these clusters is expensive and can become impractical, imposing limits on the size of models that can be trained. Several recent studies have proposed training methods that are less communication intensive, avoiding the need for a highly connected compute cluster. These state-of-the-art low communication training methods still employ a synchronization step for model parameters, which, when performed over all model replicas, can become costly on a low-bandwidth network. In this work, we propose a novel optimization method, NoLoCo, that does not explicitly synchronize all model parameters during training and, as a result, does not require any collective communication. NoLoCo implicitly synchronizes model weights via a novel variant of the Nesterov momentum optimizer by partially averaging model weights with a randomly selected other one. We provide both a theoretical convergence analysis for our proposed optimizer as well as empirical results from language model training. We benchmark NoLoCo on a wide range of accelerator counts and model sizes, between 125M to 6.8B parameters. Our method requires significantly less communication overhead than fully sharded data parallel training or even widely used low communication training method, DiLoCo. The synchronization step itself is estimated to be one magnitude faster than the all-reduce used in DiLoCo for few hundred accelerators training over the internet. We also do not have any global blocking communication that reduces accelerator idling time. Compared to DiLoCo, we also observe up to $4\%$ faster convergence rate with wide range of model sizes and accelerator counts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel optimization method called NoLoCo (No-all-reduce Low Communication) for training large language models on clusters with low-bandwidth interconnects. NoLoCo eliminates the need for explicit synchronization of model parameters through collective communication by employing a modified Nesterov momentum optimizer that partially averages model weights with randomly selected peers. The authors provide both theoretical convergence analysis and empirical results from training language models of various sizes (125M to 6.8B parameters) on different accelerator counts. They benchmark NoLoCo against fully sharded data parallel training and DiLoCo (a low-communication training method), demonstrating reduced communication overhead and, in some cases, faster convergence rates.  The paper also investigates the impact of dynamic pipeline routing on convergence and weight variance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the elimination of explicit all-reduce synchronization steps while still achieving competitive or superior convergence performance. The partial averaging of model weights via a modified Nesterov momentum optimizer is a unique approach. While decentralized training methods and modifications to momentum optimizers exist, the specific combination presented in NoLoCo appears to be a significant departure from established techniques.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the practical limitations imposed by the high communication costs of training large models, especially in environments lacking high-bandwidth interconnects. If NoLoCo's claims hold true at scale, it could potentially democratize access to large model training by making it feasible on more commodity hardware and in geo-distributed setups. The potential to scale training beyond data centers is a significant advantage.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The paper includes a theoretical convergence analysis, providing a degree of rigor.
    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Evaluation:** The experiments cover a decent range of model sizes, accelerator counts, and datasets, allowing for meaningful comparisons with existing methods.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The inclusion of an ablation study on dynamic pipeline routing provides insight into the contribution of individual components of the method.
    *   **Latency Analysis:** The latency analysis theoretically demonstrates the benefit of NoLoCo in high-latency environments.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Idealized Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis makes simplifying assumptions (e.g., independence of slow weights) that may not hold in practice, potentially limiting the applicability of the theoretical results. This needs to be thoroughly verified with empirical data.
    *   **Limited Hyperparameter Optimization:** The paper acknowledges that the hyperparameters used are optimized for FSDP and may not be optimal for NoLoCo or DiLoCo. This could underestimate the potential performance of these methods. The paper could be strengthened by demonstrating some level of hyperparameter robustness or conducting a limited hyperparameter search.
    *   **Relative Performance:** While NoLoCo often shows improvements over DiLoCo, the improvements aren't always substantial (e.g., up to 4% faster convergence). Whether this improvement is practically significant will depend on the scale and cost of training.
    *   **Lack of large scale result:** Even though results are shown for 6.8B models, there isn't evidence that the method will scale for models with trillions of parameters.
    *   **Clarity:** Some aspects of the method and analysis can be challenging to follow without deep familiarity with the related literature.

*   **Potential Impact:**  If NoLoCo proves robust and scalable, it could influence the development of future decentralized training algorithms and potentially alter the hardware requirements for large model training. It may also be interesting as a way to generate model ensembles organically.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to decentralized training. The combination of eliminating explicit synchronization and using a modified Nesterov optimizer is a compelling idea. The theoretical analysis and empirical results provide evidence of the method's potential. However, the simplifying assumptions in the theory, the limited hyperparameter optimization, and the sometimes modest performance gains temper the assessment. Furthermore, the lack of large-scale experimental results and the potential for instability prevent the paper from receiving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Breaking Bad Molecules: Are MLLMs Ready for Structure-Level Molecular Detoxification?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10912v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Lin, Ziyang Gong, Cong Wang, Yonglin Tian, Tengchao Zhang, Xue Yang, Gen Luo, Fei-Yue Wang
- **Abstract**: Toxicity remains a leading cause of early-stage drug development failure. Despite advances in molecular design and property prediction, the task of molecular toxicity repair - generating structurally valid molecular alternatives with reduced toxicity - has not yet been systematically defined or benchmarked. To fill this gap, we introduce ToxiMol, the first benchmark task for general-purpose Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) focused on molecular toxicity repair. We construct a standardized dataset covering 11 primary tasks and 560 representative toxic molecules spanning diverse mechanisms and granularities. We design a prompt annotation pipeline with mechanism-aware and task-adaptive capabilities, informed by expert toxicological knowledge. In parallel, we propose an automated evaluation framework, ToxiEval, which integrates toxicity endpoint prediction, synthetic accessibility, drug-likeness, and structural similarity into a high-throughput evaluation chain for repair success. We systematically assess nearly 30 mainstream general-purpose MLLMs and design multiple ablation studies to analyze key factors such as evaluation criteria, candidate diversity, and failure attribution. Experimental results show that although current MLLMs still face significant challenges on this task, they begin to demonstrate promising capabilities in toxicity understanding, semantic constraint adherence, and structure-aware molecule editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ToxiMol, a novel benchmark task for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) focused on molecular toxicity repair. This task requires MLLMs to generate structurally valid molecular alternatives with reduced toxicity, given the SMILES string, 2D structural image, and a natural language description of the repair objective.  The authors construct a standardized dataset, design a prompt annotation pipeline with mechanism-aware and task-adaptive capabilities, and propose an automated evaluation framework, ToxiEval, which integrates toxicity endpoint prediction, synthetic accessibility, drug-likeness, and structural similarity into an evaluation chain. They evaluate nearly 30 mainstream general-purpose MLLMs and analyze key factors through ablation studies. The results highlight challenges faced by current MLLMs, but also demonstrate promising capabilities in toxicity understanding, semantic constraint adherence, and structure-aware molecule editing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by formally defining and benchmarking the task of molecular toxicity repair for MLLMs. While individual components like toxicity prediction and molecule editing have been explored, the integrated task and the associated ToxiMol benchmark are novel. The mechanism-aware prompt annotation pipeline and the ToxiEval evaluation framework also represent notable advancements.

*   **Significance:** Toxicity is a major bottleneck in drug development, and this benchmark addresses a practically important problem. By creating a structured framework, the authors enable systematic evaluation and comparison of different MLLMs' capabilities in this domain.  The benchmark has the potential to drive further research in applying AI to drug discovery and safety.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written, clearly defining the problem, methodology, and experimental setup.
    *   The dataset is comprehensive, covering diverse toxicity mechanisms and granularities.
    *   The ToxiEval evaluation framework is rigorous, integrating multiple relevant criteria.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the factors affecting MLLM performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The overall success rates of current MLLMs on the task are still relatively low, indicating that significant improvements are needed.
    *   The ToxiEval framework, while comprehensive, is still a simulation of real-world drug development. Some of the criteria, like synthetic accessibility, are approximations.
    *   Reliance on TxGemma-Predict for safety score. The reliability of the safety score is limited by the performance of the tool, and discrepancies in performance of hERG tasks revealed potential inconsistencies in the data.
    * The analysis primarily focuses on Claude-3.7 Sonnet and may not be generalizable to all MLLMs. A more extensive analysis across all models would strengthen the findings.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field.  ToxiMol provides a much-needed benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in a practically important domain. It will likely spur further research into improving MLLM performance on toxicity repair, leading to more efficient and safer drug discovery processes.  The detailed analysis also provides useful guidance for designing more effective MLLMs for scientific applications.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper presents a clearly-defined task with a solid experimental methodology and a robust evaluation framework. It provides the community a valuable resource for measuring progress in applying MLLMs to a critical drug development challenge. Although the current performance is low, the clarity of definition, along with the evaluation framework, is a substantial advancement.

Score: 8

Rationale:
The paper defines a valuable benchmark task for MLLMs in the critical area of molecular toxicity repair. While current MLLMs still struggle, the standardized dataset, mechanism-aware prompts, and robust evaluation framework provide a solid foundation for future research and comparisons. The paper is well-executed and presents a notable advancement in the field. However, the relatively low success rates and potential dependence on TxGemma prevent a score of 9 or 10.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Foundation Models for Causal Inference via Prior-Data Fitted Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10914v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Ma, Dennis Frauen, Emil Javurek, Stefan Feuerriegel
- **Abstract**: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) have recently been proposed as a promising way to train tabular foundation models. PFNs are transformers that are pre-trained on synthetic data generated from a prespecified prior distribution and that enable Bayesian inference through in-context learning. In this paper, we introduce CausalFM, a comprehensive framework for training PFN-based foundation models in various causal inference settings. First, we formalize the construction of Bayesian priors for causal inference based on structural causal models (SCMs) in a principled way and derive necessary criteria for the validity of such priors. Building on this, we propose a novel family of prior distributions using causality-inspired Bayesian neural networks that enable CausalFM to perform Bayesian causal inference in various settings, including back-door, front-door, and instrumental variable adjustment. Finally, we instantiate CausalFM and explicitly train a foundation model for estimating conditional average treatment effects (CATEs) using back-door adjustment. We show that CausalFM performs competitively for CATE estimation using various synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks. In sum, our framework can be used as a general recipe to train foundation models for various causal inference settings. In contrast to the current state-of-the-art in causal inference, CausalFM offers a novel paradigm with the potential to fundamentally change how practitioners perform causal inference in medicine, economics, and other disciplines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CausalFM, a framework for training foundation models (specifically, prior-data fitted networks or PFNs) for causal inference. CausalFM aims to enable flexible, test-time inference in various causal settings (back-door, front-door, instrumental variables) without requiring retraining for each new task or dataset. The framework involves constructing Bayesian priors based on structural causal models (SCMs), using Bayesian neural networks to model these priors, and developing a training algorithm that leverages counterfactual data simulation. The paper demonstrates CausalFM's performance on conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimation tasks using synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks, showing competitive results compared to existing methods. The main idea is to pre-train on a large set of synthetic causal inference tasks, then perform approximate Bayesian causal inference via in-context learning on a new target task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in adapting the PFN paradigm to a broad range of causal inference problems. While PFNs have been explored in tabular settings, this work presents a comprehensive framework for incorporating SCM-based priors to handle various causal settings beyond just unconfoundedness. The theoretical formalization of constructing valid SCM-based priors and the accompanying criteria are a valuable contribution. The explicit formulation of a CausalFM prior using Bayesian neural networks is also novel. Critically, the paper differentiates itself from concurrent works proposing PFNs for causal inference by addressing identifiability concerns and offering a more general framework.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to shift the paradigm in causal inference towards more flexible and adaptable methods. Traditional causal inference methods often require task-specific model training, which can be cumbersome and computationally expensive. CausalFM offers a way to pre-train a model that can generalize to new causal tasks and datasets with minimal additional training. This is particularly useful in domains like personalized medicine, where rapid adaptation to new patient populations is crucial. Furthermore, by leveraging a Bayesian framework, CausalFM provides principled uncertainty quantification, an essential aspect of decision-making in sensitive applications. The claim that the CausalFM can choose between adjustment strategies, such as back-door, front-door, and instrumental variable adjustment, is interesting and offers potential impact.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Principled Approach:** The use of SCMs to define priors is theoretically sound and provides a clear framework for causal inference.
    * **Generality:** CausalFM is designed to handle a wider range of causal inference settings than most existing methods.
    * **Flexibility:** The in-context learning capability of PFNs enables rapid adaptation to new datasets.
    * **Uncertainty Quantification:** The Bayesian nature of CausalFM provides uncertainty estimates.
    * **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper provides empirical results comparing CausalFM to state-of-the-art methods on several synthetic and semi-synthetic benchmarks.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Synthetic Data Dependence:** The performance of CausalFM relies heavily on the quality of the synthetic data used for pre-training. The priors might be misspecified and the framework might perform poorly in real-world settings where the underlying causal relationships are more complex or the distribution of covariates is far from the synthetic data assumptions.
    * **Computational Cost:** Training large transformer-based models like PFNs can be computationally expensive.
    * **Limited Real-World Application:** The evaluation is primarily based on synthetic and semi-synthetic datasets. While this is common in causal inference research, demonstrating CausalFM's effectiveness on real-world datasets is crucial.
    * **Complexity:** Implementation and fine-tuning of SCM-based priors may be challenging for practitioners without specialized expertise.
    * **Identifiability Limitations:** Although the framework allows practitioners to set SCM priors, the correct model is rarely known in real-world settings.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of causal inference by promoting the use of foundation models and more flexible, adaptable methods. However, the real-world impact will depend on the development of more efficient training algorithms and the demonstration of effectiveness on a wider range of real-world datasets.  Further research is needed to investigate the robustness of CausalFM to different types of prior misspecification and to develop techniques for automatically learning or adapting the priors from data.

* **Justification:** The score reflects the paper's strong theoretical foundations, clear contributions, and the promising results on a range of challenging synthetic causal inference benchmarks. However, the dependence on synthetic data, computational cost and need to test on real-world datasets prevent a higher evaluation.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### M4V: Multi-Modal Mamba for Text-to-Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10915v1)
- **Authors**: Jiancheng Huang, Gengwei Zhang, Zequn Jie, Siyu Jiao, Yinlong Qian, Ling Chen, Yunchao Wei, Lin Ma
- **Abstract**: Text-to-video generation has significantly enriched content creation and holds the potential to evolve into powerful world simulators. However, modeling the vast spatiotemporal space remains computationally demanding, particularly when employing Transformers, which incur quadratic complexity in sequence processing and thus limit practical applications. Recent advancements in linear-time sequence modeling, particularly the Mamba architecture, offer a more efficient alternative. Nevertheless, its plain design limits its direct applicability to multi-modal and spatiotemporal video generation tasks. To address these challenges, we introduce M4V, a Multi-Modal Mamba framework for text-to-video generation. Specifically, we propose a multi-modal diffusion Mamba (MM-DiM) block that enables seamless integration of multi-modal information and spatiotemporal modeling through a multi-modal token re-composition design. As a result, the Mamba blocks in M4V reduce FLOPs by 45% compared to the attention-based alternative when generating videos at 768$\times$1280 resolution. Additionally, to mitigate the visual quality degradation in long-context autoregressive generation processes, we introduce a reward learning strategy that further enhances per-frame visual realism. Extensive experiments on text-to-video benchmarks demonstrate M4V's ability to produce high-quality videos while significantly lowering computational costs. Code and models will be publicly available at https://huangjch526.github.io/M4V_project.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces M4V, a Multi-Modal Mamba framework for text-to-video generation. It addresses the computational limitations of Transformer-based models, which are quadratically complex, by leveraging the linear-time complexity of the Mamba architecture. M4V proposes a multi-modal diffusion Mamba (MM-DiM) block to integrate multi-modal information and spatiotemporal modeling.  It also incorporates a reward learning strategy to improve visual quality in long-context autoregressive generation. The paper claims M4V reduces FLOPs by 45% compared to attention-based approaches at high resolutions and produces high-quality videos at lower computational cost.  Experiments on text-to-video benchmarks are presented to support these claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core novelty lies in adapting the Mamba architecture, originally designed for 1D sequence modeling, to the 3D spatiotemporal domain of video generation and addressing the multi-modal nature of text-to-video generation. The MM-DiM block, with its token re-composition strategy, is a key novel component.
    *   While adapting Mamba to vision is not entirely new (as the related work section indicates), its specific application to *text-to-video* generation with a focus on high resolution and computational efficiency is a significant contribution.
    *   The reward-based fine-tuning for autoregressive video generation is a valuable addition, as it tries to improve the quality of later frames, often suffering from error accumulation. However, the specific reward function is a combination of known metrics.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper directly tackles a major bottleneck in video generation: the high computational cost of Transformer-based models. If the claimed 45% FLOPs reduction is consistently achievable in practice without sacrificing visual quality, it represents a substantial advancement.
    *   The successful adaptation of Mamba to video generation could inspire more research into using linear-time sequence models for other computationally intensive tasks in generative AI.
    *   The paper claims to have comparable or better video quality than the current SOTA approaches.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined goals.
    *   The architecture and methodology are described in sufficient detail.
    *   The paper includes both quantitative evaluations (VBench) and a user study, which provides a more comprehensive assessment of performance.
    *   Addresses a very practical issue of computational efficiency.
    *   Ablation studies are included.
    *   Provides links to code and models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The "comparable Total Score to PyramidFlow" is a bit misleading. A closer look at Table 1 indicates the other models, which have proprietary data, are significantly higher performing in the Total Score. The authors should acknowledge more clearly that their model is only comparable to open-source Pyramidflow, when pre-trained on open-source data.
    *   The reliance on proprietary datasets like Open-Sora Plan and Midjourney images for training raises concerns about reproducibility and accessibility.
    *   The "Dynamic Degree" performance is lower than competing models. It needs to be acknowledged that their approach may hinder the diversity in generated videos.
    *   The paper mentions the choice of L:N = 1:2 for dual-stream and single-stream blocks "balances modality alignment and generation efficiency" but lacks a more thorough justification for this specific ratio.
    *   The descriptions could be more precise on various experimental details such as the hardware configurations used.
    *   The related work section is somewhat dismissive of existing Mamba-based approaches in vision, which needs to be addressed. For example, the claim that [44] uses cross-attention for multi-modal interaction should be clarified as to *how* that is different.
    *   Results in Table 1 are reproduced results (using the official implementation). It might be more beneficial if the authors evaluate their method using code implementation by previous methods as well.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   M4V has the potential to significantly influence the field by making high-resolution video generation more accessible and practical.
    *   The MM-DiM block could be a valuable building block for other multi-modal generation tasks.
    *   The reward learning strategy could be adopted in other autoregressive generation models.

*   **Score Justification:**

    I assign a **Score: 7**. This is based on the fact that the paper tackles a significant problem (computational cost of video generation) with a novel approach (Mamba adaptation). The experimental results, while somewhat limited by reliance on proprietary data, show promising results and support the claims of improved efficiency. The architectural innovations and the reward learning strategy are valuable contributions. However, the limitations regarding the lack of open-source data, the relatively weaker dynamic diversity, needs to be addressed.


- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sequential-Parallel Duality in Prefix Scannable Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10918v1)
- **Authors**: Morris Yau, Sharut Gupta, Valerie Engelmayer, Kazuki Irie, Stefanie Jegelka, Jacob Andreas
- **Abstract**: Modern neural sequence models are designed to meet the dual mandate of parallelizable training and fast sequential inference. Recent developments have given rise to various models, such as Gated Linear Attention (GLA) and Mamba, that achieve such ``sequential-parallel duality.'' This raises a natural question: can we characterize the full class of neural sequence models that support near-constant-time parallel evaluation and linear-time, constant-space sequential inference? We begin by describing a broad class of such models -- state space models -- as those whose state updates can be computed using the classic parallel prefix scan algorithm with a custom associative aggregation operator. We then define a more general class, Prefix-Scannable Models (PSMs), by relaxing the state aggregation operator to allow arbitrary (potentially non-associative) functions such as softmax attention. This generalization unifies many existing architectures, including element-wise RNNs (e.g., Mamba) and linear transformers (e.g., GLA, Mamba2, mLSTM), while also introducing new models with softmax-like operators that achieve O(1) amortized compute per token and log(N) memory for sequence length N. We empirically evaluate such models on illustrative small-scale language modeling and canonical synthetic tasks, including state tracking and associative recall. Empirically, we find that PSMs retain the expressivity of transformer-based architectures while matching the inference efficiency of state space models -- in some cases exhibiting better length generalization than either.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "sequential-parallel duality" (SPD) to characterize neural sequence models that balance parallelizable training and fast sequential inference. It identifies a class of models, called Prefix-Scannable Models (PSMs), that can be computed using a generalized prefix scan algorithm. PSMs encompass existing SPD-compatible models like Gated Linear Attention (GLA) and Mamba, while also allowing for novel architectures with non-associative aggregation rules (e.g., softmax attention). The authors show that PSMs can achieve O(1) amortized compute per token during inference with a logarithmic memory footprint. They evaluate PSMs on language modeling and algorithmic tasks, finding they retain functional effectiveness akin to transformers while achieving inference efficiency comparable to state-space models, even exhibiting superior length generalization in some instances.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of SPD is a valuable way to frame recent progress in sequence modeling. While specific architectures like GLA and Mamba exist, the paper provides a broader, unifying principle. The formalization of PSMs and the exploration of non-associative aggregators are significant contributions. The paper explicitly shows how certain existing architectures fall into the PSM framework and, crucially, points to how *new* architectures can be designed using this framework. This is a key strength.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a fundamental tension in sequence modeling: the need for parallelizable training (for scalability) and efficient sequential inference (for deployment).  By characterizing the class of models exhibiting SPD and generalizing them as PSMs, the paper offers a roadmap for future architecture design.  The demonstration of PSMs with non-associative aggregation, particularly with amortized O(1) inference cost, is a promising avenue for exploration. Showing that PSMs can outperform existing models on specific tasks such as length generalization is a valuable finding. However, the empirical evaluation is limited to small-scale tasks, which is a significant weakness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear formalization of SPD and PSMs.
    *   Unification of diverse existing architectures under a common framework.
    *   Identification of a promising design space with non-associative aggregators.
    *   Demonstration of strong length generalization in experiments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited empirical evaluation: The tasks are illustrative but not necessarily representative of real-world sequence modeling challenges (e.g., large-scale language modeling). Scaling the models up is crucial.
    *   While the theoretical framework is compelling, the practical advantages of non-associative PSMs over highly optimized existing architectures (e.g., Mamba) need to be more convincingly demonstrated on a broader range of tasks.
    *   The paper could benefit from more discussion of the limitations imposed by the prefix scan structure and the trade-offs involved. While the theory points to promising designs, the paper does not offer insights into practical challenges of implementation (e.g., numerical stability or implementation complexity of static scan or online counters)
    *    The paper does not address potential limitations when computing non-associative operators and the impact these have on performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by providing a clear framework for designing and analyzing sequence models with SPD. It highlights a promising direction with non-associative PSMs, which could lead to the development of more efficient and effective architectures. It also deepens the connections with efficient parallel algorithms like prefix scan. However, more extensive empirical validation is needed to solidify its impact.

*   **Justification:** The score is based on the paper's novelty in providing a unifying framework and identifying a new design space, tempered by the limited empirical evidence. While the conceptual contributions are strong, the lack of large-scale experiments and a more nuanced discussion of limitations hinders the paper's overall impact at this stage.

### Decomposing MLP Activations into Interpretable Features via Semi-Nonnegative Matrix Factorization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10920v1)
- **Authors**: Or Shafran, Atticus Geiger, Mor Geva
- **Abstract**: A central goal for mechanistic interpretability has been to identify the right units of analysis in large language models (LLMs) that causally explain their outputs. While early work focused on individual neurons, evidence that neurons often encode multiple concepts has motivated a shift toward analyzing directions in activation space. A key question is how to find directions that capture interpretable features in an unsupervised manner. Current methods rely on dictionary learning with sparse autoencoders (SAEs), commonly trained over residual stream activations to learn directions from scratch. However, SAEs often struggle in causal evaluations and lack intrinsic interpretability, as their learning is not explicitly tied to the computations of the model. Here, we tackle these limitations by directly decomposing MLP activations with semi-nonnegative matrix factorization (SNMF), such that the learned features are (a) sparse linear combinations of co-activated neurons, and (b) mapped to their activating inputs, making them directly interpretable. Experiments on Llama 3.1, Gemma 2 and GPT-2 show that SNMF derived features outperform SAEs and a strong supervised baseline (difference-in-means) on causal steering, while aligning with human-interpretable concepts. Further analysis reveals that specific neuron combinations are reused across semantically-related features, exposing a hierarchical structure in the MLP's activation space. Together, these results position SNMF as a simple and effective tool for identifying interpretable features and dissecting concept representations in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a rigorous critical evaluation, including a novelty and significance score.

**Summary**

The paper introduces a new method for decomposing MLP activations in large language models (LLMs) into interpretable features using Semi-Nonnegative Matrix Factorization (SNMF).  Unlike dictionary learning with sparse autoencoders (SAEs), which learn features from scratch and can struggle with causal evaluations, this approach directly decomposes MLP activations into sparse linear combinations of co-activated neurons. The key benefits are that the learned features are mapped to activating inputs, making them directly interpretable and grounded in the model's mechanisms.  Experiments on Llama 3.1, Gemma 2, and GPT-2 demonstrate that SNMF-derived features outperform SAEs and a supervised difference-in-means baseline on causal steering. Analysis further reveals that specific neuron combinations are reused across semantically-related features, suggesting a hierarchical structure in the MLP's activation space. The authors posit that SNMF offers a simple and effective tool for dissecting concept representations in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying SNMF directly to MLP activations isn't entirely groundbreaking, as NMF and its variants have been used in deep learning before, including for activations in other contexts (e.g., images, and even residual stream activations). The novelty lies primarily in *specifically applying it to MLP layers, framing features as sparse linear combinations of co-activated neurons grounded in MLP weight matrices,* and *demonstrating its effectiveness against existing unsupervised methods (SAEs) and supervised baselines in concept steering and interpretability.*  The connection to a hierarchical structure and the observation that neuron combinations are re-used across concepts is a solid incremental contribution.

*   **Significance:** Mechanistic interpretability is a very active area of research, and any tool that helps understand how LLMs represent concepts is potentially significant.  The advantages highlighted over SAEs—specifically, the improved causal steering performance, the groundedness in the model's computations (MLP weight matrices), and better interpretability through direct mapping to inputs—address some of the existing limitations in the field. However, the results, while promising, are primarily empirical. There's a lack of theoretical grounding that would explain *why* SNMF works better than SAEs in this specific context.  The hierarchical discovery is a significant result in terms of identifying the building blocks that compose more abstract concepts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined goals.
    *   The method is relatively simple and computationally efficient (compared to training SAEs).
    *   The experimental results show a clear improvement over established baselines in concept steering tasks.
    *   The qualitative analysis revealing hierarchical structure and neuron reuse is compelling.
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited theoretical explanation of *why* SNMF works better.
    *   The novelty, while present, is somewhat incremental rather than a paradigm shift.
    *   The benchmark experiments are somewhat limited in scope (limited models, limited concepts), though the models are of a reasonable size.
    *   Reliance on GPT-4o-mini for description generation introduces a potential for bias and subjectivity. The quality of these descriptions directly impacts the evaluation, so it adds another degree of freedom.
    *   While the paper highlights groundedness in the MLP's mechanisms, the features learned are still *directions* in activation space. It remains an open question how these directions causally influence model behavior in complex scenarios beyond concept steering.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could encourage researchers to explore alternative decomposition methods besides SAEs and to focus on methods that are more intrinsically linked to the model's computational graph. It could also stimulate further research into the hierarchical organization of knowledge within LLMs. The simplicity of SNMF might make it a more accessible tool for interpretability research.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses an important problem and presents promising results, the improvements are incremental, and the theoretical grounding is lacking. The reliance on external models (e.g. GPT-4o-mini) also introduces a potential source of noise. However, the hierarchical discovery has the potential to be significant, and it opens up a new avenue to analyze model building blocks. The experimental validation is solid, and the method is reasonably straightforward to implement. It also showcases that the model uses building blocks to form concepts, which has significance for the field. Given these factors, I think a score of 6 reflects a reasonable assessment.

### Robustly Improving LLM Fairness in Realistic Settings via Interpretability
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10922v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Karvonen, Samuel Marks
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes hiring applications, making decisions that directly impact people's careers and livelihoods. While prior studies suggest simple anti-bias prompts can eliminate demographic biases in controlled evaluations, we find these mitigations fail when realistic contextual details are introduced. We address these failures through internal bias mitigation: by identifying and neutralizing sensitive attribute directions within model activations, we achieve robust bias reduction across all tested scenarios. Across leading commercial (GPT-4o, Claude 4 Sonnet, Gemini 2.5 Flash) and open-source models (Gemma-2 27B, Gemma-3, Mistral-24B), we find that adding realistic context such as company names, culture descriptions from public careers pages, and selective hiring constraints (e.g.,``only accept candidates in the top 10\%") induces significant racial and gender biases (up to 12\% differences in interview rates). When these biases emerge, they consistently favor Black over White candidates and female over male candidates across all tested models and scenarios. Moreover, models can infer demographics and become biased from subtle cues like college affiliations, with these biases remaining invisible even when inspecting the model's chain-of-thought reasoning. To address these limitations, our internal bias mitigation identifies race and gender-correlated directions and applies affine concept editing at inference time. Despite using directions from a simple synthetic dataset, the intervention generalizes robustly, consistently reducing bias to very low levels (typically under 1\%, always below 2.5\%) while largely maintaining model performance. Our findings suggest that practitioners deploying LLMs for hiring should adopt more realistic evaluation methodologies and consider internal mitigation strategies for equitable outcomes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Robustly Improving LLM Fairness in Realistic Settings via Interpretability":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of prompt-based fairness mitigations in Large Language Models (LLMs) when applied to realistic hiring scenarios.  The authors find that simple anti-bias prompts effective in controlled settings fail when contextual details like company names, culture descriptions, and selective hiring constraints are introduced. This induces significant racial and gender biases in both commercial and open-source LLMs. They also show that models can infer demographics from subtle cues like college affiliations, making anonymization strategies less effective. The paper then proposes an internal bias mitigation technique: identifying and neutralizing sensitive attribute directions within model activations using affine concept editing. This method robustly reduces bias across all tested realistic scenarios while largely maintaining model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's most significant contribution is demonstrating the fragility of prompt-based fairness techniques in more realistic, contextualized settings. Prior work has shown the effectiveness of these techniques in simpler scenarios, but this paper clearly exposes their limitations. The application of internal bias mitigation strategies, while not entirely new in the broader interpretability literature, is novel in the context of mitigating fairness issues in LLMs specifically for high-stakes applications like hiring. The use of a synthetic dataset to identify bias directions and then applying them to real-world resume data to test generalizability adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The findings have important practical implications. As LLMs are increasingly used in hiring, understanding the limitations of current mitigation strategies and developing more robust methods is crucial for ensuring fair and equitable outcomes. The internal bias mitigation technique presented provides a promising alternative.  The study's focus on real-world context and use cases (e.g., specific company cultures, selective hiring criteria) increases its practical relevance. The paper also highlights the inadequacy of chain-of-thought monitoring in detecting bias, further adding to its significance.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study evaluates multiple LLMs (both commercial and open-source) across diverse and realistic settings, making the findings more generalizable.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and defines the limitations of prompt-based methods in realistic scenarios.
    *   **Effective Mitigation Strategy:** The proposed internal mitigation technique is shown to be robust and effective in reducing bias without significantly degrading model performance.
    *   **Interpretability-Inspired Approach:** The use of interpretability methods to identify and neutralize bias is a promising direction for future research.
    *   **Relevance to a high-stakes application:** The focus on fairness in hiring and its downstream implications is compelling.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplified Representation of Complex Processes:** The paper still simplifies a complex hiring pipeline.  While more realistic than previous studies, it does not capture the full nuances of human decision-making in hiring processes.
    *   **Limited Scope of Protected Characteristics:** The study focuses primarily on race and gender.  While important, there are other protected characteristics (age, disability, etc.) that deserve attention.
    *   **Potential for Unintended Consequences:** The internal mitigation strategy, while effective in the scenarios tested, might have unintended consequences on other aspects of model behavior.  The authors acknowledge the potential impact on MMLU and propose further research on refinement techniques to minimize performance trade-offs, highlighting this consideration.
    *   **Dependence on Synthetic Data for Bias Direction:** While the authors argue that this choice enables generalizability, it is important to be cautious. One potential concern is that the bias directions learned on a synthetic dataset may not perfectly capture the nuances of real-world biases, which are complex and influenced by diverse social factors. Furthermore, reliance on name-based demographic signals may oversimplify the complexities of identity and representation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on fairness in LLMs. It will encourage the development of more robust evaluation methodologies and motivate the exploration of internal mitigation strategies. The findings will also inform practitioners deploying LLMs in high-stakes applications, urging them to move beyond simple prompt-based mitigations.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by highlighting the limitations of existing fairness mitigation techniques in realistic settings and proposing a robust alternative. It's well-executed, clearly written, and addresses an important practical problem. The study's comprehensiveness, use of real-world contexts, and effectiveness of the proposed mitigation strategy are strong points. However, it would be strengthened by addressing some of the limitations, such as incorporating additional protected characteristics and exploring the long-term effects of the internal mitigation strategy. It is also important to be critical of the study's simplification of highly nuanced social phenomena and potential reliance on overly simplistic demographic signals. Nevertheless, its findings are significant and warrant a high score, reflecting the paper's novelty, rigor, and potential impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### The Role of Generative AI in Facilitating Social Interactions: A Scoping Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10927v1)
- **Authors**: T. T. J. E. Arets, G. Perugia, M. Houben, W. A. IJsselsteijn
- **Abstract**: Reduced social connectedness increasingly poses a threat to mental health, life expectancy, and general well-being. Generative AI (GAI) technologies, such as large language models (LLMs) and image generation tools, are increasingly integrated into applications aimed at enhancing human social experiences. Despite their growing presence, little is known about how these technologies influence social interactions. This scoping review investigates how GAI-based applications are currently designed to facilitate social interaction, what forms of social engagement they target, and which design and evaluation methodologies designers use to create and evaluate them. Through an analysis of 30 studies published since 2020, we identify key trends in application domains including storytelling, socio-emotional skills training, reminiscence, collaborative learning, music making, and general conversation. We highlight the role of participatory and co-design approaches in fostering both effective technology use and social engagement, while also examining socio-ethical concerns such as cultural bias and accessibility. This review underscores the potential of GAI to support dynamic and personalized interactions, but calls for greater attention to equitable design practices and inclusive evaluation strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a scoping review of Generative AI (GAI) applications designed to facilitate social interactions. The authors analyze 30 studies published since 2020, exploring the types of GAI applications used (e.g., large language models, image generators), the kinds of social interactions they target (storytelling, socio-emotional training, reminiscence, etc.), and the design and evaluation methodologies employed. The review highlights the potential of GAI to support personalized and dynamic interactions, emphasizing participatory design and co-design approaches. It also addresses socio-ethical concerns related to biases and accessibility and calls for more equitable design practices and inclusive evaluation strategies. The paper concludes that GAI is predominantly unimodal (text-based) and that more multimodal designs should be considered.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The review's novelty lies in its systematic exploration of GAI's relatively understudied role in fostering social connectedness. While technology's broader influence on social interaction is well-established, the focus on emerging GAI tools and their specific design for facilitating connection provides a timely and unique perspective. The paper identifies gaps in the research (e.g., limited multimodal engagement, uneven end-user control, a focus on system metrics rather than social meaning) and proposes actionable recommendations.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the growing recognition of social disconnectedness as a public health issue. By mapping the landscape of GAI-based solutions, the review contributes to the development of interventions that can address this problem. Furthermore, the paper's identification of ethical and design considerations, such as bias and user involvement, is crucial for ensuring that GAI technologies promote inclusive and equitable social interactions.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Approach:** The scoping review methodology is appropriate for this emerging research area. The use of PRISMA-ScR guidelines enhances the rigor and transparency of the review process.
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper covers a broad range of GAI applications and social interaction contexts, providing a holistic overview of the field.
    *   **Critical Analysis:** The authors critically examine the included studies, identifying limitations and proposing areas for future research. The discussion of ethical considerations and user involvement is particularly valuable.
    *   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper offers practical guidance for researchers and designers, emphasizing the importance of participatory design, equitable access, and comprehensive evaluation metrics.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Depth:** Scoping reviews are, by nature, broad rather than deep. While the paper provides a valuable overview, it does not delve deeply into the theoretical underpinnings of social connectedness or the specific mechanisms through which GAI technologies influence social interaction.
    *   **Potential Publication Bias:** Focusing solely on published studies might introduce some bias, as unpublished or negative findings are not captured.
    *   **Rapidly Evolving Field:** GAI is rapidly changing, so the snapshot provided by this review, although current, may quickly become outdated. A stronger emphasis on forecasting/predicting future directions could bolster the paper's long-term impact.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper demonstrates a solid understanding of both the technical and social aspects of GAI, providing a well-structured and critical analysis of existing research. It is a highly relevant study and provides a clear foundation for future research and development in the field. The ethical considerations and design recommendations enhance the practical value of the review. While some limitations related to the nature of scoping reviews and the fast-paced evolution of GAI exist, they do not significantly detract from the paper's overall contribution. It is also clear that there is a lack of long-term studies or studies in real-world environments, potentially impacting the paper's overall impact.
