### Dynamic Epistemic Friction in Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10934v1)
- **Authors**: Timothy Obiso, Kenneth Lai, Abhijnan Nath, Nikhil Krishnaswamy, James Pustejovsky
- **Abstract**: Recent developments in aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences have significantly enhanced their utility in human-AI collaborative scenarios. However, such approaches often neglect the critical role of "epistemic friction," or the inherent resistance encountered when updating beliefs in response to new, conflicting, or ambiguous information. In this paper, we define dynamic epistemic friction as the resistance to epistemic integration, characterized by the misalignment between an agent's current belief state and new propositions supported by external evidence. We position this within the framework of Dynamic Epistemic Logic (Van Benthem and Pacuit, 2011), where friction emerges as nontrivial belief-revision during the interaction. We then present analyses from a situated collaborative task that demonstrate how this model of epistemic friction can effectively predict belief updates in dialogues, and we subsequently discuss how the model of belief alignment as a measure of epistemic resistance or friction can naturally be made more sophisticated to accommodate the complexities of real-world dialogue scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "dynamic epistemic friction" (DEF) in dialogues, defined as the resistance encountered when updating beliefs due to new, conflicting, or ambiguous information. It frames this within the context of Dynamic Epistemic Logic (DEL) and proposes a vector-based model utilizing Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR) to represent agents' belief states and propositions. This allows for geometric measures (e.g., cosine similarity) to quantify the alignment between beliefs and new information, thereby estimating epistemic friction. The paper then presents analyses from a situated collaborative task (the Weights Task) to demonstrate how this model can predict belief updates and how adjusting parameters related to the magnitude of the friction affects performance. Ultimately, the goal is to iteratively reduce friction in a discourse in order to guide participants toward a better epistemic equilibrium.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific application of epistemic friction within the DEL framework, coupled with the use of vector-based representations to model and quantify this friction in a dynamic dialogue setting. While the individual components (DEL, HRR, epistemic friction) aren't entirely novel in isolation, their combination and application to modeling belief updates *specifically* in collaborative dialogues is a unique contribution. Using vector representations in this way has not been fully explored, particularly for capturing the dynamics of belief updates over multiple turns. The introduction of "friction coefficients" to tune the model's behavior is also a nice touch, although not fundamentally ground breaking. The iterative update procedure that focuses on reducing friction in a discourse is of value.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial aspect of human-AI interaction that is often overlooked: the non-trivial nature of belief updates and the resistance to integrating new information. By providing a computational model of epistemic friction, the paper offers tools to analyze and potentially improve human-AI collaboration, particularly in scenarios involving uncertainty, conflicting information, or strategic deception. This can lead to more robust and adaptive AI systems that are better equipped to handle the complexities of human communication. The Weights Task is not the most general task however, limiting the direct applicability of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Conceptualization:** The paper provides a clear and well-defined notion of dynamic epistemic friction, grounding it in existing literature and formal frameworks.
    *   **Formal Model:** The proposed vector-based model is mathematically sound and offers a practical approach to quantifying and reasoning about belief updates and resistance.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results, although limited to the Weights Task dataset, provide evidence that the model can effectively predict belief updates and that tuning friction parameters can improve performance.
    *   **Potential Applications:** The paper highlights several potential applications of the model in areas such as human-AI collaboration, dialogue systems, and understanding strategic communication.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Empirical Scope:** The empirical evaluation is confined to a single dataset (the Weights Task), which limits the generalizability of the results. The task, while useful for controlled experimentation, is somewhat artificial and might not fully capture the complexities of real-world dialogues.
    *   **Simplified Belief Representation:** The vector-based representation of beliefs, while computationally tractable, is a simplification of the underlying cognitive processes. It may not capture all the nuances of human belief systems. A more sophisticated approach to encoding proposition would improve the model.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Baselines:** The paper would benefit from comparing the proposed model to other existing approaches for belief tracking or dialogue management to better demonstrate its advantages. A simple, interpretable baseline, even if not state of the art, could highlight the value of the friction coefficients.
    *   **Unclear Scalability:** It is not clear how well the model scales to more complex dialogues with a larger number of participants, propositions, and evidence sources.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas, including:
    *   **Human-AI Interaction:** By providing a framework for understanding and mitigating epistemic friction, the paper can contribute to the design of more effective and collaborative AI systems.
    *   **Dialogue Systems:** The model can be used to develop dialogue systems that are more sensitive to the beliefs and resistance of their users, leading to more natural and engaging conversations.
    *   **Computational Pragmatics:** The paper can inspire further research on the interplay between logical inference, pragmatic reasoning, and belief revision in dialogue.

*   **Overall:** While the paper has some limitations in its empirical scope and model complexity, it presents a novel and potentially significant contribution to the field of dialogue modeling and human-AI interaction. The concept of dynamic epistemic friction provides a valuable lens for analyzing belief updates and the challenges of achieving common ground in communication.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:** The paper introduces a novel concept and combines existing methods in a unique and promising way (DEL, HRR, epistemic friction and coefficients). The empirical results provide preliminary support for the model's effectiveness. However, the limited empirical scope, simplified belief representation, and lack of comparisons to baselines hold it back from a higher score. The reliance on the WTD dataset also limits the immediate, general applicability of the research. A more complex and realistic study that can directly capture the effects of the friction coefficients on discourse state is necessary before the full utility of the model can be demonstrated. Despite these shortcomings, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to inspire further research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Self-Adapting Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10943v1)
- **Authors**: Adam Zweiger, Jyothish Pari, Han Guo, Ekin Akyürek, Yoon Kim, Pulkit Agrawal
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are powerful but static; they lack mechanisms to adapt their weights in response to new tasks, knowledge, or examples. We introduce Self-Adapting LLMs (SEAL), a framework that enables LLMs to self-adapt by generating their own finetuning data and update directives. Given a new input, the model produces a self-edit-a generation that may restructure the information in different ways, specify optimization hyperparameters, or invoke tools for data augmentation and gradient-based updates. Through supervised finetuning (SFT), these self-edits result in persistent weight updates, enabling lasting adaptation. To train the model to produce effective self-edits, we use a reinforcement learning loop with the downstream performance of the updated model as the reward signal. Unlike prior approaches that rely on separate adaptation modules or auxiliary networks, SEAL directly uses the model's own generation to control its adaptation process. Experiments on knowledge incorporation and few-shot generalization show that SEAL is a promising step toward language models capable of self-directed adaptation. Our website and code is available at https://jyopari.github.io/posts/seal.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Self-Adapting Language Models (SEAL), a framework enabling LLMs to adapt to new tasks and knowledge by generating their own finetuning data and update directives (self-edits). Given an input, the model produces a "self-edit"—instructions for restructuring information, specifying optimization hyperparameters, or invoking tools for data augmentation and gradient-based updates. These self-edits are then used for supervised finetuning, leading to lasting adaptation. A reinforcement learning loop trains the model to produce effective self-edits, using the downstream performance of the updated model as the reward signal. The approach is evaluated on knowledge incorporation and few-shot generalization tasks, demonstrating promising results for self-directed adaptation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of LLMs generating their own training data and adaptation instructions is novel and addresses a significant limitation of static LLMs. The approach differs from existing methods that rely on separate adaptation modules or auxiliary networks by directly using the model's generation to control its own adaptation process. The integration of reinforcement learning to optimize the self-edit generation process adds further novelty. The exploration of different self-edit prompts and their impact contributes to the understanding of adaptation strategies.

*   **Significance:**  The ability for LLMs to self-adapt has immense significance for several reasons. First, it addresses the data scarcity problem, allowing models to learn effectively from limited data. Second, it paves the way for continual learning, enabling models to integrate new information over time without catastrophic forgetting. Third, it reduces reliance on human-designed adaptation strategies, potentially leading to more efficient and effective learning. The experiments on knowledge incorporation and few-shot generalization provide concrete evidence of the potential of SEAL.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Conceptual Innovation:** The paper presents a clearly articulated and original concept with a well-defined framework.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:**  The experimental setup is thorough, including comparisons against strong baselines and ablations.
    *   **Practical Impact:**  The demonstrated results on knowledge incorporation and few-shot learning suggest that SEAL can be a practical tool for improving the performance of LLMs in real-world applications.
    *   **Well-written and Accessible:** The paper is well-written, making the approach and results easy to understand.
    *   **Code and Resources Availability**: Facilitates reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges the high computational cost of the TTT reward loop, which might limit its applicability in resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Catastrophic Forgetting:**  While the paper addresses the issue of catastrophic forgetting, it acknowledges that SEAL is still susceptible to it. More research is needed to fully address this limitation.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** The experimental evaluation focuses on two specific domains (knowledge incorporation and few-shot learning). Further research is needed to evaluate the generalizability of SEAL to other tasks and domains. The selection of 11 training ARC tasks raises concerns about potential bias; this needs to be addressed with more varied tasks.
    *   **Baseline Performance:** The selection of the initial model performance on ARC might have affected the results and made the method look better. It is important to discuss how the model's performance will vary with a higher performing initial model.
    *   **Reward Design:** The reward design is still simplistic. More complex reward shaping techniques could further improve performance.
    *   **Synthetic vs. Real Data:** Despite outperforming GPT-4 generation on some datasets, it remains to be seen if this will generalize to more complicated datasets.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLMs by inspiring new research on self-adaptation and continual learning. The SEAL framework can serve as a foundation for future research on developing more efficient and effective adaptation strategies for LLMs. It encourages a shift from static to dynamic LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and promising approach to enabling LLMs to self-adapt, addressing a key limitation of existing models. The conceptual innovation, rigorous evaluation, and potential practical impact make this a significant contribution to the field. However, the computational cost, limited scope of experiments, and the remaining challenges related to catastrophic forgetting prevent it from achieving a higher score.

### GUARD: Guided Unlearning and Retention via Data Attribution for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10946v1)
- **Authors**: Evelyn Ma, Duo Zhou, Peizhi Niu, Huiting Zhou, Huan Zhang, Olgica Milenkovic, S. Rasoul Etesami
- **Abstract**: Unlearning in large language models (LLMs) is becoming increasingly important due to regulatory compliance, copyright protection, and privacy concerns. However, a key challenge in LLM unlearning is unintended forgetting, where the removal of specific data inadvertently impairs the utility of the model and its retention of valuable, desired information. While prior work has primarily focused on architectural innovations, the influence of data-level factors on unlearning performance remains underexplored. As a result, existing methods often suffer from degraded retention when forgetting high-impact data. To address this, we propose GUARD-a novel framework for Guided Unlearning And Retention via Data attribution. At its core, GUARD introduces a lightweight proxy data attribution metric tailored for LLM unlearning, which quantifies the "alignment" between the forget and retain sets while remaining computationally efficient. Building on this, we design a novel unlearning objective that assigns adaptive, nonuniform unlearning weights to samples, inversely proportional to their proxy attribution scores. Through such a reallocation of unlearning power, GUARD mitigates unintended losses in retention. We provide rigorous theoretical guarantees that GUARD significantly enhances retention while maintaining forgetting metrics comparable to prior methods. Extensive experiments on the TOFU benchmark across multiple LLM architectures demonstrate that GUARD substantially improves utility preservation while ensuring effective unlearning. Notably, GUARD reduces utility sacrifice on the Retain Set by up to 194.92% in terms of Truth Ratio when forgetting 10% of the training data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GUARD, a novel framework for guided unlearning and retention in large language models (LLMs). GUARD leverages a lightweight proxy data attribution metric tailored for LLM unlearning to quantify the "alignment" between the forget and retain sets. It then designs an unlearning objective that assigns adaptive, non-uniform unlearning weights to samples based on their proxy attribution scores. This approach aims to mitigate unintended losses in retention.  The authors provide theoretical guarantees and empirical results on the TOFU benchmark demonstrating improved utility preservation while ensuring effective unlearning.  Notably, GUARD reduces utility sacrifice on the Retain Set compared to Gradient Ascent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its **data-attribution-driven** approach to LLM unlearning. While data attribution has been explored in other machine learning contexts, its application to LLM unlearning with a focus on balancing forgetting and retention, along with the proposed lightweight proxy attribution metric, is a valuable contribution.  The introduction of temperature-controlled reverse unification to control the variance of unlearning weights is also a novel element.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of unintended forgetting during LLM unlearning is a critical one. The ability to selectively remove information while preserving valuable knowledge is essential for practical unlearning solutions. GUARD tackles this challenge directly and offers a promising approach. The empirical results, showing substantial improvements in utility preservation over existing methods on a standard benchmark (TOFU), are compelling.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Approach:**  The GUARD framework is grounded in a clear theoretical motivation and supported by rigorous analysis. The theoretical guarantees, though relying on simplifying assumptions, provide valuable insights into the behavior of the method.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The proposed proxy data attribution metric is designed to be computationally efficient, making it suitable for LLMs.  The empirical results demonstrate that GUARD can be implemented without excessive overhead.
    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results on the TOFU benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of GUARD across different LLM architectures and evaluation protocols.  The consistent improvements over baselines, particularly in terms of utility preservation, are significant.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the concepts and methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Assumptions:** The theoretical analysis relies on several simplifying assumptions (e.g., small updates, isotropic gradients, knowledge entanglement).  While these assumptions are common in data attribution work, it limits the theoretical guarantees and the generalizability of the theoretical analysis.
    *   **Proxy Metric:** The proxy attribution metric captures alignment between forget sample and retain dataset, but might not fully reflect the real influence of a sample on model predictions, as it measures the direction alignment, and not the impact magnitude on retained dataset.
    *   **Evaluation Scope:** While the TOFU benchmark is a valuable resource, the experimental results are still limited to that benchmark. It needs evaluation across more datasets with different characteristics to generalize the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM unlearning. The data-attribution-driven approach offers a promising direction for developing more selective and effective unlearning methods.  The lightweight proxy attribution metric could also inspire the development of other computationally efficient techniques for analyzing and manipulating LLMs.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper introduces a valuable contribution to the field of LLM unlearning, addressing a practical challenge with a novel, grounded in theoretical analysis, and well empirically supported method, its reliance on strong assumption and somewhat limited evaluation hinders the impact for real world applications. The proxy metric is potentially limited and need further investigation and refinement, and the empirical evaluation is needs more generalization to real world datasets.

**Score: 7**

- **Score**: 7/10

### Execution Guided Line-by-Line Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10948v1)
- **Authors**: Boaz Lavon, Shahar Katz, Lior Wolf
- **Abstract**: We present a novel approach to neural code generation that incorporates real-time execution signals into the language model generation process. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive code generation capabilities, they typically do not utilize execution feedback during inference, a critical signal that human programmers regularly leverage. Our method, Execution-Guided Classifier-Free Guidance (EG-CFG), dynamically incorporates execution signals as the model generates code, providing line-by-line feedback that guides the generation process toward executable solutions. EG-CFG employs a multi-stage process: first, we conduct beam search to sample candidate program completions for each line; second, we extract execution signals by executing these candidates against test cases; and finally, we incorporate these signals into the prompt during generation. By maintaining consistent signals across tokens within the same line and refreshing signals at line boundaries, our approach provides coherent guidance while preserving syntactic structure. Moreover, the method naturally supports native parallelism at the task level in which multiple agents operate in parallel, exploring diverse reasoning paths and collectively generating a broad set of candidate solutions. Our experiments across diverse coding tasks demonstrate that EG-CFG significantly improves code generation performance compared to standard approaches, achieving state-of-the-art results across various levels of complexity, from foundational problems to challenging competitive programming tasks. Our code is available at: https://github.com/boazlavon/eg_cfg
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Execution Guided Line-by-Line Code Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach, EG-CFG (Execution-Guided Classifier-Free Guidance), to neural code generation.  EG-CFG integrates real-time execution signals into the language model generation process. Unlike traditional LLMs that primarily rely on static code representations, EG-CFG leverages execution feedback during inference, mimicking how human programmers debug code.  The method involves a multi-stage process:  beam search to generate candidate code completions for each line, executing these candidates against test cases, and incorporating execution signals (traces) into the prompt using Classifier-Free Guidance. EG-CFG also supports parallel agents that allow exploration of diverse reasoning paths.  Experiments on MBPP, HumanEval, and CodeContests benchmarks demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to standard approaches, establishing new state-of-the-art results on several of these benchmarks using open-source LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty in several aspects:

    *   **Dynamic Execution Feedback:** The core idea of dynamically incorporating execution traces *during* the code generation process (line-by-line) is a valuable contribution. This is in contrast to prior work that uses execution for post-hoc refinement or self-debugging after an entire code block is generated.

    *   **Classifier-Free Guidance Integration:** Applying CFG conditioned on execution feedback is a clever way to guide the generation process towards executable solutions while maintaining syntactic plausibility.  The careful crafting of the prompts that leverage execution traces to influence generation is a strong point.

    *   **Parallelism:** The introduction of native parallelism through multiple agents with different hyperparameter settings further sets this work apart from most iterative refinement methods.

*   **Significance:** The results clearly indicate the significance of the approach. Achieving state-of-the-art results on several established benchmarks, especially when using open-source LLMs (like DeepSeek) to outperform methods using closed-source models (like GPT-4), highlights the value of the proposed technique.  The improvements on the more challenging MBPP-ET and HumanEval-ET datasets are particularly noteworthy, suggesting better generalization capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across multiple benchmarks, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of EG-CFG.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The description of the EG-CFG approach, including the different stages, dynamic signal aggregation, and CFG integration, is clear and well-explained.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of different components of the proposed approach (beam search, CFG, detailed traces).
    *   **Open Source:** The availability of the code contributes to reproducibility and allows for further research and adoption of the technique.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The computational overhead introduced by the approach, while mitigated by parallel execution, is a definite limitation. More efficient methods for extracting and incorporating execution signals would be beneficial.
    *   **Reliance on Test Cases:** The method's effectiveness is contingent on the quality and coverage of available test cases. This might be a limitation in scenarios where comprehensive test cases are difficult to obtain or generate. The paper clearly states this limitation, which is a positive attribute.
    *   **Bottom-Up Approach:** The paper notes that the inference loop is bottom-up and does not exploit task decomposition, suggesting an area for future improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The EG-CFG approach has the potential to significantly influence future research in neural code generation and LLM-based programming.  It demonstrates the value of integrating real-time feedback into the generation process, potentially leading to more robust and reliable code generation systems.  The parallel agent framework is also a promising direction for exploration.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of neural code generation. The approach is novel, the results are strong, and the methodology is well-explained. While there are some limitations related to computational cost and reliance on test cases, the benefits of EG-CFG in terms of improved accuracy and the use of open-source LLMs are undeniable. The novelty lies in the combination of line-by-line execution feedback with CFG, and the parallel agent framework, which clearly results in performance gains. EG-CFG significantly advances the state-of-the-art, especially considering it beats previous methods that relied on closed-source LLMs.

### Build the web for agents, not agents for the web
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10953v1)
- **Authors**: Xing Han Lù, Gaurav Kamath, Marius Mosbach, Siva Reddy
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and multimodal counterparts have spurred significant interest in developing web agents -- AI systems capable of autonomously navigating and completing tasks within web environments. While holding tremendous promise for automating complex web interactions, current approaches face substantial challenges due to the fundamental mismatch between human-designed interfaces and LLM capabilities. Current methods struggle with the inherent complexity of web inputs, whether processing massive DOM trees, relying on screenshots augmented with additional information, or bypassing the user interface entirely through API interactions. This position paper advocates for a paradigm shift in web agent research: rather than forcing web agents to adapt to interfaces designed for humans, we should develop a new interaction paradigm specifically optimized for agentic capabilities. To this end, we introduce the concept of an Agentic Web Interface (AWI), an interface specifically designed for agents to navigate a website. We establish six guiding principles for AWI design, emphasizing safety, efficiency, and standardization, to account for the interests of all primary stakeholders. This reframing aims to overcome fundamental limitations of existing interfaces, paving the way for more efficient, reliable, and transparent web agent design, which will be a collaborative effort involving the broader ML community.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've carefully analyzed the paper "Build the web for agents, not agents for the web." Here's a summary, a critical evaluation, and a score.

**Summary:**

This paper argues that current approaches to building web agents (AI systems that autonomously navigate websites) are fundamentally flawed because they force agents to adapt to interfaces designed for humans. The authors propose a paradigm shift: instead of making agents fit the existing web, the web should be redesigned to better suit agents. They introduce the concept of an "Agentic Web Interface" (AWI), an interface specifically optimized for agentic capabilities. They outline six guiding principles for AWI design, emphasizing safety, efficiency, and standardization, and argue that collaboration from the broader ML community is essential for success.  They touch on the relationship to existing protocols like Model Context Protocol (MCP), emphasizing that AWIs are distinct as interfaces providing a stateful representation of websites.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of designing web interfaces specifically for agents is novel and timely. While the idea of agents needing different interfaces has been hinted at previously, this paper explicitly articulates it as a necessary paradigm shift and provides a framework to think about it. The term "Agentic Web Interface" is a new and useful label for this concept. Prior work often focuses on improving agent architectures for *existing* websites, but this paper flips the script.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper lies in its potential to redirect research efforts in the web agent space. By highlighting the mismatch between human-centric interfaces and agent needs, it encourages researchers to consider alternative interaction paradigms.  If adopted, this could lead to more efficient, reliable, and safe web agents.  The paper effectively identifies current limitations, lays out key design principles, and points to areas where the broader ML community can contribute. It also opens up new avenues for collaboration between web developers and AI researchers.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem with current approaches to web agent design.
    *   **Compelling Argument:** The argument for a paradigm shift is well-reasoned and persuasive.
    *   **Useful Framework:** The six guiding principles for AWI design provide a concrete starting point for future research.
    *   **Emphasis on Collaboration:** The paper emphasizes the importance of involving the broader ML community, which is crucial for the success of this endeavor.
    *   **Relating to Relevant Fields:** Effectively links to many different areas of machine learning.
    *   **Good Structure:** The structure of the paper is clear and logical.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Concreteness:** The paper deliberately avoids providing a specific AWI implementation, which, while understandable, makes it somewhat abstract. It remains a high-level vision rather than a concrete solution.
    *   **Overly Optimistic:** The paper might be slightly overoptimistic about the willingness of website owners to adopt AWIs.  Incentivizing website owners to adopt AWIs is briefly mentioned, but the challenges in doing so in the real world will likely be substantial.
    *   **Unclear adoption path:** The paper describes that websites and web agents will somehow come together on an AWI. It would be useful to provide an example of how this would unfold to a user on a specific website in an actual scenario.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the lack of a concrete implementation, the paper's conceptual contribution is valuable. It effectively identifies a critical problem in the field of web agent research and proposes a promising new direction. The emphasis on a paradigm shift and the articulated guiding principles are significant contributions in themselves. While it doesn't offer solutions, it frames the *problem* more clearly and provides a vision for where the *solutions* should be sought. The weaknesses identified temper the evaluation to some extent; especially the lack of specifics. Still, this paper serves a vital function by identifying the root of the problem and providing directions to solve it, therefore it should not be discounted based solely on the lack of a detailed implementation.

### SWE-Factory: Your Automated Factory for Issue Resolution Training Data and Evaluation Benchmarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10954v1)
- **Authors**: Lianghong Guo, Yanlin Wang, Caihua Li, Pengyu Yang, Jiachi Chen, Wei Tao, Yingtian Zou, Duyu Tang, Zibin Zheng
- **Abstract**: Constructing large-scale datasets for the GitHub issue resolution task is crucial for both training and evaluating the software engineering capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the traditional process for creating such benchmarks is notoriously challenging and labor-intensive, particularly in the stages of setting up evaluation environments, grading test outcomes, and validating task instances. In this paper, we propose SWE-Factory, an automated pipeline designed to address these challenges. To tackle these issues, our pipeline integrates three core automated components. First, we introduce SWE-Builder, a multi-agent system that automates evaluation environment construction, which employs four specialized agents that work in a collaborative, iterative loop and leverages an environment memory pool to enhance efficiency. Second, we introduce a standardized, exit-code-based grading method that eliminates the need for manually writing custom parsers. Finally, we automate the fail2pass validation process using these reliable exit code signals. Experiments on 671 issues across four programming languages show that our pipeline can effectively construct valid task instances; for example, with GPT-4.1-mini, our SWE-Builder constructs 269 valid instances at $0.045 per instance, while with Gemini-2.5-flash, it achieves comparable performance at the lowest cost of $0.024 per instance. We also demonstrate that our exit-code-based grading achieves 100% accuracy compared to manual inspection, and our automated fail2pass validation reaches a precision of 0.92 and a recall of 1.00. We hope our automated pipeline will accelerate the collection of large-scale, high-quality GitHub issue resolution datasets for both training and evaluation. Our code and datasets are released at https://github.com/DeepSoftwareAnalytics/swe-factory.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SWE-Factory paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SWE-Factory, an automated pipeline for creating and validating GitHub issue resolution training data and evaluation benchmarks. The system tackles the traditionally labor-intensive process by automating environment construction, grading test outcomes, and validating task instances. The core components are: (1) SWE-Builder, a multi-agent system leveraging LLMs to automatically construct evaluation environments; (2) a standardized, exit-code-based grading method; and (3) automated fail2pass validation using exit codes. Experiments on a dataset of 671 issues across Python, Java, JavaScript, and TypeScript demonstrate the pipeline's effectiveness and cost efficiency.  The paper shows that SWE-Builder can construct valid task instances with various LLMs at low cost, that the exit-code grading is accurate, and that automated fail2pass validation achieves high precision and recall. The paper also identifies and characterizes the "error2pass" phenomenon, highlighting its importance in benchmark construction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a significant contribution to the automation of benchmark creation for software engineering tasks, specifically for GitHub issue resolution. While individual components like using LLMs for code generation or analyzing test logs aren't entirely novel on their own, the *integration* of these technologies into a comprehensive and fully automated pipeline is a key strength.  The SWE-Builder multi-agent system is a well-designed approach that leverages LLMs intelligently.  The exit-code-based grading and automated fail2pass validation are particularly effective simplifications that avoid the need for complex custom parsers, significantly reducing manual effort. The identification and characterization of the error2pass phenomenon is a valuable contribution to the methodology of benchmark construction and addresses limitations in existing validation approaches. The combination of these automated components to tackle the complex problem of benchmark creation is a significant contribution of the paper.

*   **Significance:** The construction of high-quality benchmarks is crucial for advancing research in software engineering and for reliably evaluating the capabilities of LLMs in this domain. SWE-Factory has the potential to significantly accelerate the development and validation of such benchmarks by reducing the manual effort and expertise required. The system's modular design and open-source release encourage further development and adoption by the community. The cost-effectiveness and language-agnostic design also make the system attractive for creating benchmarks in areas where resources are limited or expertise is scarce. The identification and handling of the "error2pass" cases further ensures the reliability of the generated benchmarks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Automation:**  SWE-Factory automates the complete benchmark creation pipeline, from environment setup to validation.
    *   **Cost Efficiency:**  The system achieves good performance with relatively low-cost LLMs.
    *   **Accuracy:**  The exit-code-based grading method is highly accurate and avoids the need for custom parsers.
    *   **Robustness:** The fail2pass validation effectively identifies valid benchmark instances while also flagging problem cases for further inspection.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the system's design, implementation, and evaluation.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The open-source release ensures reproducibility and facilitates future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM Dependency:**  The system's performance is reliant on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. While the paper demonstrates effectiveness with cost-effective LLMs, the system's overall quality may be limited by the performance of available models, which may change significantly over time.
    *   **Limited Language Support:**  While the system supports four languages, expanding the language coverage to include more niche or less-well-supported languages may present additional challenges.
    *   **Error2Pass Cases:** While the automated system identifies these cases, it doesn't fully address how the models perform on these instances or propose methods for repair; further research would strengthen this area. The handling of these issues could also be improved by either discarding instances or creating separate metrics for evaluating models on error2pass instances.
    *   **Dataset Bias**: It would be beneficial to provide more details on the potential biases in the source repositories and if SWE-Factory manages to account for those biases during instance creation.

*   **Impact:** SWE-Factory has the potential to significantly impact the field by accelerating the development of high-quality benchmarks for GitHub issue resolution. This, in turn, can improve the evaluation of software engineering capabilities of LLMs and facilitate further research in this area. The open-source release and modular design will also encourage community adoption and further development of the system.

*   **Score Justification:**
Given the well-designed system architecture, comprehensive automation, cost-effectiveness, validation of key components, and potential to accelerate benchmark creation for software engineering, a score of 8 is justified. The paper's strengths in fully automating and simplifying a challenging and complex task outweigh its weaknesses in LLM dependency and the limitations of current error2pass handling. The identification and handling of error2pass instances, in particular, demonstrates the authors’ rigor in ensuring benchmark quality.

### ReGuidance: A Simple Diffusion Wrapper for Boosting Sample Quality on Hard Inverse Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10955v1)
- **Authors**: Aayush Karan, Kulin Shah, Sitan Chen
- **Abstract**: There has been a flurry of activity around using pretrained diffusion models as informed data priors for solving inverse problems, and more generally around steering these models using reward models. Training-free methods like diffusion posterior sampling (DPS) and its many variants have offered flexible heuristic algorithms for these tasks, but when the reward is not informative enough, e.g., in hard inverse problems with low signal-to-noise ratio, these techniques veer off the data manifold, failing to produce realistic outputs. In this work, we devise a simple wrapper, ReGuidance, for boosting both the sample realism and reward achieved by these methods. Given a candidate solution $\hat{x}$ produced by an algorithm of the user's choice, we propose inverting the solution by running the unconditional probability flow ODE in reverse starting from $\hat{x}$, and then using the resulting latent as an initialization for DPS. We evaluate our wrapper on hard inverse problems like large box in-painting and super-resolution with high upscaling. Whereas state-of-the-art baselines visibly fail, we find that applying our wrapper on top of these baselines significantly boosts sample quality and measurement consistency. We complement these findings with theory proving that on certain multimodal data distributions, ReGuidance simultaneously boosts the reward and brings the candidate solution closer to the data manifold. To our knowledge, this constitutes the first rigorous algorithmic guarantee for DPS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces REGUIDANCE, a simple two-step "wrapper" algorithm to enhance the performance of diffusion-based methods for solving hard inverse problems, particularly those with low signal-to-noise ratios (SNR).  The first step inverts a candidate solution using the probability flow ODE to obtain a latent representation.  The second step initializes Diffusion Posterior Sampling (DPS) with this latent representation to generate an improved reconstruction. The authors demonstrate empirically that REGUIDANCE boosts sample quality and measurement consistency in tasks like box in-painting and super-resolution, where state-of-the-art baselines struggle.  The paper also presents theoretical guarantees for REGUIDANCE's performance on Gaussian mixture data, proving that it improves both reward and realism, and that the performance is highly dependent on the quality of the initial latent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using the latent space for inverse problems is present in literature. The specific two-step approach of reverse ODE followed by DPS is novel, as is its application to hard inverse problems, as well as the demonstration that an *improved* initial latent is key for performance and the theoretical guarantees presented. The analysis is also important and well described.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a key limitation of current diffusion-based inverse problem solvers: their failure in low-SNR scenarios.  REGUIDANCE provides a practical method to improve performance in such settings.  The theoretical analysis, while limited to Gaussian mixtures, offers valuable insights into the behavior of DPS and the importance of latent initialization. The finding that SDE based DPS performs worse also carries significance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Simplicity:** The algorithm is easy to implement and integrate with existing inverse problem solvers.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The paper provides comprehensive experimental results on challenging tasks.
    * **Theoretical Justification:**  The theoretical analysis, though in a simplified setting, provides a deeper understanding of the method's behavior and provides a framework for future analyses of similar approaches.
    * **Addresses Real Limitation:** The paper tackles a real-world limitation of diffusion models.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Scope:**  The theoretical results are limited to Gaussian mixture data, which might not fully capture the complexity of real-world image distributions.
    * **Dependency on Initial Solution:** While REGUIDANCE improves existing solutions, its performance still relies on the quality of the initial reconstruction. The quality is very dependent on the initial latent, which requires further investigation of how to better extract or construct.
    * **Computational Cost:** Reverse step and subsequent DPS step does add computational overhead to existing pipelines which could hinder adoption.
* **Potential Influence:** REGUIDANCE could influence future research by focusing attention on the importance of latent initialization for diffusion-based inverse problem solvers, and provide a foundation for further theoretical analysis. It is well positioned to become a default approach for researchers in the field to use and reference, given its plug and play characteristic.

**Justification for Score:**

REGUIDANCE addresses a practical limitation of diffusion models with a simple and effective algorithm and a robust theoretical foundation that helps to justify the approach and demonstrate what limitations it can overcome. However the dependency on the initial solution/latent and the computational complexity limit it slightly.
